# Status history

Pocketdesk's build journal, one entry per item or milestone, newest first. It was the
*Current Status* section of `AGENTS.md` until 2026-10-22, when it moved here to keep
what every session loads small. `git log` holds the commit-level detail.

Add each new entry at the top, at the milestone level.

- 2026-10-31: **Linux firewall: nftables and iptables read, and said at pairing.** Item 1 of the plan after 0.8.3. In the 2026-10-31 device batch the laptop's firewall dropped every LAN connection but ssh, yet Diagnostics had said "no firewall found" the day before: it read only ufw and firewalld (O9), and the laptop runs plain nftables (Arch's stock `/etc/nftables.conf` lets in ssh only too). **Reading:** a new `core::local::netfilter` reads nftables (`nft list ruleset`, or the file it loads) and iptables (`iptables-save`'s form) into chains and rules, and walks a new IPv4 packet from the network (not loopback, a container bridge or a VPN) through every input base chain: jumps, gotos, returns, named sets, defines, service names, `ct state`, interfaces and the policy. A rule it cannot judge where it could decide makes the answer "unsure", never "open": the row says the firewall may be blocking. Without root the rules running cannot be read, so the app reads `/etc/nftables.conf` (or `/etc/iptables/iptables.rules`, `rules.v4`) while its service is enabled or active; Arch's `nftables.service` is a oneshot that reads "inactive" once it has loaded them at boot, which the laptop showed. **The row** asks for the control and stream ports (red when shut), KDE Connect's 1714-1764 with its bridge on and mDNS 5353 with `lan_discovery` on (a warning), names Arch's stock rules, and its hint gives the exact command for ufw, firewalld, nftables (`nft 'insert rule <the chain that drops> …'`, which goes above the stock reject line, and the lines to keep in `/etc/nftables.conf`) or iptables. ufw and firewalld are read for KDE Connect and mDNS too (ufw's "KDE Connect" app profile, firewalld's `kdeconnect` and `mdns` services). **At pairing:** the Pair page and Settings → Other computers, which said a Windows Public network, now say on Linux when the firewall shuts (or may shut) the control or stream port, read off the UI thread every 30 s. No new setting: what is asked follows the KDE Connect and "Found on this network" settings already there. **Tests:** fixtures for Arch's stock file and its `nft list ruleset` form, ports opened by rules, sets and defines, Debian's accept-all file, a drop above an accept, jump and goto chains, a rule not understood, an include, IPv6-only and loopback rules, an iptables DROP ruleset, ufw and firewalld with and without KDE Connect and mDNS. **On the laptop**, read-only: the live probe without root reads its rules file and says red for all four, with the nft lines, and the Pair page's line; its `nft list ruleset` read with root gives the same. Owed: §246.
  - **Review round** (request changes: one Critical, five Important, six Nits). C1: a verdict map on a key other than `dport` (`ip protocol vmap`, `meta l4proto vmap`, `iifname vmap`) was read as a rule with no verdict, so a drop in it could read as open; every vmap is now a rule that may decide ("may be blocking"), and `ct state vmap` is read exactly (a new packet takes `new`'s verdict). I1: rules read from the file are never green ("the rules file lets phones in; the running rules need root to read", a warning). I2: filter chains on prerouting and ingress, and iptables' raw and mangle PREROUTING, count as input does. I3: the walk is iterative (a stack of chains jumped to, at most 64 deep and 10,000 rules, then unsure), tested on a 50,000-address blocklist. I4: the nft hint names every chain found keeping the ports out, iptables' with its table, and says what it assumes when none was found. I5: an iptables `-m` match not read is a condition. N1: rate limits (nft `limit`, iptables `-m limit`, `hashlimit`) make a rule may-hold. N2: Arch's stock name only when ssh is in and the two ports shut. N3: ufw's mDNS reads "may block" (its before.rules rule is not shown). N4: iptables is asked when nftables gives no verdict, rules that cannot be read without a boot service say so (information) instead of "no firewall found", systemctl answers are kept a minute and one reading serves both pages for 20 s. N5: the guide says the verdict is for a phone on the same network. N6: which firewall is judged is a pure `choose_linux_firewall`, tested.
- 2026-10-31: **Crash leftovers of state saves are swept at start.** Follow-up to the safe-replace item (its re-review's N6 and N7). Each save of a single state file writes `<file>.<pid>.<n>.tmp` beside it (`atomic_file::temp_beside`), and unlike the old fixed `*.tmp` name nothing reuses one a crash left, so a copy of the file's data (a trust store, clip history, phone notes) could sit there for good. **Fix:** when `pocketdesk-server start` runs, `paths::sweep_temp_leftovers` goes through the state and config folders once and deletes only regular files named `<known state file>.<digits>.<digits>.tmp` (the known names are `paths::is_temp_beside_file`: the files the saves above write, and `view-<n>.json`) that were last written at least 10 minutes ago and whose pid is not this process's; folders, links and every other name stay, including a young temp file (a save in flight in the desktop app). **Tests:** old leftovers of known files go, and unknown stems, near-miss shapes (missing or non-numeric parts, extra parts, other suffixes, a prefix on the name), this process's own, a young one and a folder with a leftover's name all stay; a missing folder is nothing; the known-name predicate. **Also (N7):** `trust_store_path`'s doc comment is back on it (it had stayed above `off_the_workers`), and the `//` comments over the `WRITING` mutexes in `answers.rs` and `computers.rs` are doc comments again, with no stray blank lines.
- 2026-10-31: **State files save on Windows while another program holds them.** The I2 finding of the hermetic-tests review. Every state file (the trust store, `computers.json`, device keys, config, the phone mirrors, …) is saved by writing a temp file and renaming it over the old one; on Windows that rename fails with "Access is denied" (or a sharing or lock violation) while another program holds the file or the temp file open without `FILE_SHARE_DELETE` (an antivirus scan, the Search indexer, a backup or sync client, an editor), so a pairing, an unpair or a remote revoke failed (the revoked device stayed trusted) and left a `*.tmp` copy of the data behind. **Fix:** one helper, `pocketdesk_core::atomic_file::replace(tmp, dest)`, now takes all 72 rename-over-a-state-file sites in core and the server (57 files): on Windows it retries errors 5, 32 and 33 with a bounded backoff (10 to 200 ms, about half a second in all) and on the last failure removes the temp file and returns the error. The rename stays `std::fs::rename`, which on Windows (Rust 1.99) is `MoveFileExW(MOVEFILE_REPLACE_EXISTING)` and, on access denied, a second try with `FileRenameInfoEx` and POSIX semantics that replaces a target held open with `FILE_SHARE_DELETE`; calling `MoveFileExW` or `ReplaceFileW` directly would lose that second try and fail on the same sharing errors. Unix is unchanged (`rename(2)`, and the sites that fsync the temp file still do). **Readers:** ours all open through `std::fs`, whose Windows default share mode already includes `FILE_SHARE_DELETE`, and nothing in the tree sets a custom `share_mode`, so no reader changed; a test shows an open std reader never blocks a save. **Left alone** (not saves of a fresh temp file): moving a damaged `device_keys.json` aside, claiming a computer request (`computers.rs`, which must fail if withdrawn), the event log's rotation, the state-folder migration (`paths.rs`), a finished transfer's `.part` (`plugins/share.rs`), and two test fixtures. **Tests:** the retry policy with an injected rename (retried then lands; bounded, and the temp file goes; any other error fails at once), the codes counted only on Windows, a real replace and a failed one; on Windows, a handle opened with `FILE_SHARE_READ` alone refuses the plain rename and `replace` waits it out, a handle that never lets go fails within the bound and leaves no temp file, and a trust-store revoke lands while such a handle is held for 200 ms with no `*.tmp` left. **Verified:** on Windows, fmt, workspace clippy, the client-profile and bridge clippy, and the workspace tests (2739 passed); on the Arch laptop, fmt, workspace and client-profile clippy, and the workspace tests.
  - **Review round** (approve with changes: no Critical, two Important, five Nits). I1: the saves that ran on a Tokio worker in async code (the live-session mirror, the phones' batteries and media, a kept phone notification, the WAN status, a drive send's sent-list entry) go through `spawn_blocking`, and the pairing's trust-store save through a new `state::off_the_workers` (`block_in_place` on the server's multi-thread runtime, inline on a current-thread one, where it would panic); the saves under a file's own `WRITING` mutex (and the known-peers `PINS_FILE`) keep it, since it keeps that file's writes in turn, with the possible half-second hold said there and in the helper's docs. I2: error 5 is retried only when it can clear: the target is looked at once on the first one, and a folder or a read-only file fails at once. N1: the docs say what std's Windows rename does. N2: the 28 saves of a single state file (`computers.json`, the phone mirrors, the live-session mirror and the rest) take a temp name of their own (`atomic_file::temp_beside`: `<file>.<pid>.<n>.tmp`), so one process's failed save never removes another's temp file; the per-item files in watched folders (approvals, the outbox, typing, …) keep their names, which those folders' sweeps parse, and have one writer each. N3: three leftover second removals went. N4: the revoke test's holder lets go after 100 ms. **Verified:** on Windows, fmt, workspace, client-profile and bridge clippy, and the workspace tests (2742 passed); on the Arch laptop, fmt, workspace and client-profile clippy, and the workspace tests.

Tagging and the steps after publishing are the owner's.
- 2026-10-31: **Hermetic server tests.** On Windows a full test run failed now and then in `plugins::pair::owner_mark_tests`, `service::vector_tests` and `peer_computers`, each green alone. **Cause:** every server lib test that reaches the state folder shares one per process (`pd-server-tests-<pid>`), so tests running side by side wrote the same trust store, PIN and device keys: on Windows, replacing `trusted_devices.json` while another thread had it open failed with "Access is denied", and changes made outside `TrustStore::update` were lost (a stress loop of the pairing, service and link tests failed 2 of 15; 1 of 5 full server runs failed 8 tests). **Fix:** `state::use_test_dirs()` now returns a guard that holds the folder for the test that took it (a per-thread turn that nests on the test's thread, `#[must_use]`, and fails after 120 s naming the test holding it); every test that reaches the folder takes it in its own body and keeps it, including the eight a trace found reaching it without one, and a helper returns it with what it made (`temp_home`) or asserts the caller's (`assert_test_turn`). The identity test's three pair-and-retry tries went with the race they hid. Outside tests nothing changes. **Guard:** a core `test-harness` feature, turned on only from the server's and the desktop app's `[dev-dependencies]` (resolver 2 keeps it out of every release, package and Android bridge build; a CI step checks with `cargo tree`), turns a Cargo test binary (`target/<profile>/deps/<crate>-<16 hex>`) that resolves the state or config folder with no override to throwaway `pd-harness-<pid>` folders (said once on stderr, those older than a day swept) instead of the owner's (`%LOCALAPPDATA%\pocketdesk`, `~/.local/state/pocketdesk`); core's own tests still fail loudly, an exported `POCKETDESK_STATE_DIR`/`POCKETDESK_CONFIG_DIR` still wins, and the server's `state::state_dir()` sets its test folders first. **Also:** core's iroh loopback test endpoint reserved a port, let it go and bound it again, so a parallel test could take it ("address in use"); it binds port 0 and reads the port back. The link privacy test waits up to 5 s (a deadline, not a count), not 1 s, for its release on the blocking pool. **Verified:** on Windows, 5 of 5 full server runs green (`cargo test -p pocketdesk-server -j 2`; before, 1 of 5 failed), 29 of 30 stress runs of the pairing, service, link and remote-devices tests at 16 threads green (the one failure was WSAENOBUFS, ephemeral ports used up by the back-to-back loop, not shared state; before, 2 of 15 failed), the full workspace tests (2723 passed), core with `wan`, fmt and all three clippy profiles; on the Arch laptop, Linux clippy over the workspace, 5 of 5 server runs and core with `wan` green. After the review round (the feature gate, the 120 s wait, helpers keeping the guard, one try in the identity test), 5 of 5 server runs, the workspace tests (2728 passed), fmt and all clippy profiles green on Windows; Linux clippy, 5 of 5 server runs, the desktop tests and the `cargo tree` check (no `test-harness` outside tests) green on the laptop. A test-thread trace found no other test reaching the folder without its turn. The lib suite's time is unchanged (about 33 s). Six test processes in a workspace run had been resolving the folders with no override, which meant the owner's; they now get `pd-harness-<pid>`. Still open, for its own item: on Windows the trust store's replace (and the other rename-over saves) fails while any reader has the file open, which the server and the desktop app could meet in real use too.
- 2026-10-31: **The wheel through KDE's portal scrolls a notch, not a pixel.** The open wheel item of §100, left by the KDE host check below. **Why:** the server told the RemoteDesktop portal every wheel notch as `NotifyPointerAxisDiscrete` steps. GNOME handles that call as the spec means it (mutter makes each step a wheel click with its source), but xdg-desktop-portal-kde hands the steps to KWin's `org_kde_kwin_fake_input.axis` as an axis value, so a notch is 1 pixel. Its `NotifyPointerAxis` takes the same route with pixels, sending `-dy` as the vertical value (the sign turned over) and `dx` as it is. **Fix:** on KDE (`XDG_CURRENT_DESKTOP` naming `KDE`, or `KDE_FULL_SESSION` without a name) a notch is `NotifyPointerAxis` at 15, the axis value libinput and KWin give a mouse notch and what uinput's `REL_WHEEL` and the wlroots path (`wlr_input::NOTCH`) scroll by, in KDE's signs and with `finish` set; everywhere else the steps stay. The choice and mapping are plain functions (`portal_input::wheel_style`, `wheel_call`) with unit tests, and the probe gained `hscroll:STEPS` and prints the style. **Measured on KDE** (Plasma 6.8.2's `kwin_wayland --virtual` with xdg-desktop-portal-kde in a fresh throwaway nspawn container on the laptop, only the Intel render node bound in, pre-authorized; the probe sent a notch down, one up, three down, one right and one left into a `wev` window): before, `axis` 1.0, -1.0, 3.0, 1.0, -1.0; after, 15.0, -15.0, 45.0, 15.0, -15.0, the right way each time. Neither carries `value120` or an axis source (KDE's fake input has neither), so an app that scrolls only by `value120` still won't. **Not done:** the wire carries whole notches only (the desktop window rounds a touchpad's points to notches first), so there is no finer scroll to send smoothly; GNOME was not run (its portal dialog cannot be passed headless, and mutter's handling of the steps is unchanged), so the GNOME wheel stays owed in §100.

- 2026-10-31: **KDE Plasma host, checked headless.** Item 6 of the plan after 0.8.2 (Reddit asked about KDE; owed check §100). **The rig:** a throwaway Arch container on the laptop (`systemd-nspawn` on the Arch bootstrap image, as podman and docker are not installed; only the Intel render node bound in, no input device, no host D-Bus or Wayland socket) ran Plasma 6.8.2's `kwin_wayland --virtual` (1280x720) with xdg-desktop-portal 1.22.1, xdg-desktop-portal-kde 6.8.2, PipeWire 1.6.10, `wev` and an animated QML window. A new ignored probe (`core/tests/portal_live.rs`, development-setup §3a) drove the portal capture as the server does: it counted frames, sent input, and snapshotted the screen to answer the dialog by script. **Found and fixed:** (1) the portal capture never got a frame: its PipeWire stream offered no `EnumFormat`, so the link never negotiated (the stream went active and stayed empty); it now offers BGRx or BGRA at any size and rate, preferring the configured ones, and got about 58 fps; (2) the negotiated size was never read, as `param_changed` compared against id 0 instead of `SPA_PARAM_Format` (4); (3) with input wanted, an unanswered RemoteDesktop dialog fell back to a second, ScreenCast dialog, two minutes before failing; no answer now ends it as a cancel does (60 s); (4) the server called the portal unnamed ("an unidentifiable application", its grants kept for every unnamed program alike); it now registers `dev.pocketdesk.Pocketdesk` with the portal's host registry, the desktop file the .deb and AUR install (a test holds the id to both). **Measured on KDE:** the dialog shows and cannot be passed headless on its own (60 s, then the error); answered once (its screen ticked, then Enter), "Remember the screen-sharing grant"'s ScreenCast token restored the next session in 0.1 s with no dialog; Plasma's pre-authorization (`kde-authorized`, `remote-desktop`, as KRdp uses; the guide's new "KDE Plasma: no dialog at all") started a RemoteDesktop session with no dialog, both for the unnamed probe (the empty id pre-authorized) and, once named, for `dev.pocketdesk.Pocketdesk` (that id pre-authorized), and taking it back brought the dialog again; a RemoteDesktop token restored with the pre-authorization taken back; input through the portal reached a `wev` window (pointer moves, a left click, the `a` key, `é` by keysym). **Not fixed:** a wheel notch through KDE's portal arrives as a 1-pixel scroll (`axis` 1.0, no `value120`), so the phone's wheel barely scrolls on KDE without uinput. KWin's privileged path (`zkde_screencast_unstable_v1` and `org_kde_kwin_fake_input` through `X-KDE-Wayland-Interfaces`) was not needed: the portal works unattended once allowed. Still owed at a real Plasma desk: a session from the phone, the systemd unit, two phones; and all of GNOME.
  - **Review round** (request changes: one Critical, four Important, six Nits). C1: the probe named the capture module under CI's core profiles that lack `capture`; it is now `cfg(all(linux, capture))`. I1: an unanswered RemoteDesktop dialog falls back to the screen alone again when a ScreenCast grant is kept (it restores with no dialog), and only without one ends after the minute, saying someone must allow it once. I2: the portal keeps grants by app id, so **after updating from 0.8.2 GNOME and KDE ask once more** even with the grant remembered; the guide says so, and a Start that took over 5 s with a kept token logs that the grant was not taken. I3: the tray's desktop file (written to name it to the GlobalShortcuts portal) hid the packaged menu entry behind a `NoDisplay` stub naming the tray; it now writes nothing where a package installed one, removes its own (marked, or the old stub byte for byte) once one is, never touches the owner's, and otherwise writes a marked entry that starts the app. I4: the guide and SECURITY.md (limit 24) warn that allowing Pocketdesk ahead lets any program running as you claim the name, and give `flatpak permission-remove`. Nits: a chunk is read from its offset, a bad stride is packed rows and a corrupted chunk is dropped; a failed stream (no format agreed) is logged and ends the capture; the input capture's own D-Bus connection is named too, and no name is claimed in a sandbox; the probe's snapshots go only into its throwaway folder. Other formats (RGBx for some wlroots portals) wait: GNOME and KDE offer BGRx.

- 2026-10-31: **One pairing for all my devices: my computers introduce the phone (ecosystem phase 4).** Plan item 2. A phone paired with computer A's own code (remote revoke's owner mark) asks A which of A's computers paired both ways it could pair with too (`kdeconnect.pocketdesk.introduce` `list`); its owner taps Pair on "Also pair with <B>?" (`pair`); A asks B on their link (`introduce_phone`, naming the phone's id, the TLS and iroh keys bound to it at A and A's permissions for it); B, with "Let my computers add my phones here" on (`computer_introductions`, on by default), answers with a PIN of its own bound to that id and TLS key, single use, 120 s (`introduce_phone_answer`, with B's facts); A hands the phone a pairing link made of the PIN, B's addresses and the certificate and endpoint A pinned for B (`introduce_answer`); the phone pairs as from a scanned code, and B decides again under its files' locks before it trusts the phone as an owner phone. **Decisions:** B gives B's defaults for a new owner phone narrowed to A's permissions for it (never more than either; never BK5's keyboard and mouse onto the phone), with the owner mark; no profile is copied (an owner phone has none). Removal follows the introduction by default: each computer keeps `introduced` (phone, computer) in `computers.json`, and a phone removed on one however it went is owed to the other (`owed_removals`) as remote revoke's `remove`, sent on the link, retried, and refused there only by that computer's own "Let my devices remove others from afar", which the removing computer then says. Refusals: `not_owner`, `not_mine`, `not_linked`, `off`, `taken` (an id paired either way, a listed computer, B's or A's own id, or bound to another key), `no_key`, `busy`, `no_answer` (an older B), `failed`. Paced: 30 lists a minute and 5 introductions per 10 minutes per phone at A, 5 per 10 minutes per computer and 4 PINs waiting at B. Old ends drop the new packet types (no plugin). Where: phone Home after pairing and the card's Manage → "Pair with my other computers…" (`IntroduceSheet`, `IntroduceRule`), phone Settings "Offer my other computers after pairing" with the last outcome, desktop Settings → Other computers. SECURITY limit 72; protocol.md §2.8. Tests: every refusal on both sides, the PIN bound to id, key, once and time, the re-decision as the phone pairs (switch off, id taken, introducer revoked since), the permissions given, the link built from what A pinned, A to B to the phone's pairing end to end in one process with two state folders, owed removals (owing, sending, retrying, dropping, no echo back, forgotten with the computer), the phone's rule (JVM). Owed: everything on devices, checklist §244. **Review round (PR #581):** I1, A sends B a cancel of the waiting PIN when the phone is removed before it paired (at once, or with the owed removal, which B answers by dropping the PIN), and a phone's wrong PINs count against its introduction PIN alone, discarded after 3, never the owner's own PIN; I2, the phone at B is also held to what B turned off for A, an introduced phone is never introduced onward (`introduced_owners` in the trust store, `is_own_code_owner`), and when A is unpaired at B or no longer paired both ways the phones A introduced lose the owner mark (staying paired) and B says so, SECURITY 72 rewritten to say what is and is not bounded; N1, an owed removal is marked sent only once it went, and one dropped unsent (30 days, or its computer unpaired) is said; N2, a missing or unreadable trust store owes nothing; N3, A keeps an introduction only once B confirms the pairing (`paired`), and B takes an owed removal (`introduced: true`) only for a phone introduced between the two; N4, refused introductions are paced and a computer replaces only its own waiting PIN; N5, a drop guard takes a waiting introduction out however the wait ends, and the pairing ask is answered off the connection's loop; N6, tests through `handle_pair` (pairs on its key, refuses another key and `computer: true`, misses never charge the owner's PIN), `send_owed_with`, `answered`, and a Kotlin test of the link taken; N7, the TLS-only road and these behaviours documented.
- 2026-10-31: **Virtual screens on Hyprland.** The phone's second screen (T10j) worked on Sway only; it now works on Hyprland 0.56 too, the owner's compositor. A spike on the laptop showed `output create headless <name>` and `output remove` are not caught by the broken `dispatch`/`keyword` (hyprwm/Hyprland#16224), and that `eval` with real Lua (`hl.monitor({…})`) sets the size without `keyword`. The server names each screen `PD-<tag>-<n>` (the tag eight hex digits from its state folder, so a throwaway beside the real server never touches the other's; with no state folder it adds none), sets its rule first so the output comes up at its size, places it with `auto-right` (a fixed position moved the laptop's own `auto` monitor), and removes only its own tagged outputs. Review fixes: adds run one at a time from the limit checks to the record, so a phone sending many at once gets one screen and the computer at most four; once `output create` is sent, every failure removes the output; only a checked name and sides reach the Lua; the start-up sweep runs before phones can ask, waited for at most 5 s (and every Hyprland socket connect, write and read is time-limited), so a hung compositor cannot hold the server's start back; a Hyprland older than 0.56 is named in the refusal. Hyprland 0.56 cannot drop a monitor rule, so one stays per screen name until it reloads its config. Its record now names the compositor instance (an old `sway` field is still read), and a server that starts removes its tagged outputs that record does not list, which covers a crash between making a screen and saving it. Both apps' Settings name Hyprland; no new setting. Unit tests for naming, the Lua rule, replies and parsing; a live `#[ignore]` test adds a 1080x2400 screen, captures it at that size and removes it; a throwaway server swept a planted leftover. Owed: the whole flow from a phone (checklist §245). Left for later: reusing it to fit another computer's screen to a window.

"Used a while ago" from 7 days on stays as chosen in UX1 (N12's note). PR #580's review: a divider keeps Remove apart from Rename, Manage is a button with the platform's expand and collapse actions, a window chip no longer repeats its text as a description, and the font-scale hook's release guard is tested; a stamp for a card whose address moved mid-session is dropped, which is safe (N2, left as is). - 2026-10-31: **One version across my devices.** Item 4 of the plan after 0.8.2: three devices update by three roads (AUR or a .deb, winget or setup.exe, F-Droid or Obtainium) and most features need both ends, yet nothing said which release each ran. **On the wire:** the identity carries `pocketdeskVersion` beside `deviceName` (an extra field: KDE Connect and older Pocketdesk ignore it and send none). **Compared by number:** `core/src/version.rs` cleans a claimed release as a name is cleaned (digits first, letters, digits and `.+-_`, at most 32 characters) and compares `major.minor.patch` only, so a `-dev` or `+<git hash>` build is the release it names and never nags; the phone's `PeerVersion.kt` keeps the same rules, held by tests on both sides. **Kept:** the server keeps a paired phone's or computer's in the trust store (`versions`, gone with the device, written only when it changes) and says it in `pocketdesk devices --json`; a computer that dials another's link keeps that one's in `computers.json` (`versions`); the bridge keeps each computer's per dialed address, forgotten when an identity says none or the computer is let go. **Shown:** the desktop's Devices page and Settings → Other computers say "runs 0.8.1" on the row, and when it is not this computer's release "older than this one: update it" or "newer than this one: update this one" in the warning colour; the phone's card on Home says the same of the computer under its status line ("than this app"). Each app's "Tell me when my devices run different versions" (desktop `version_hints`, Settings → Phones; phone Settings → Pairing; on) turns the advice off and keeps the release shown. No GitHub call, no update check, no telemetry: only the owner's devices are compared, and nothing is gated on it. Tests: the comparison and cleaning in core and Kotlin, the identity round trip (packet, handshake and the internet road's), the trust store and `computers.json` keeping and dropping it, the server noting it, the local API, the bridge's per-address cache, desktop kittests for both rows and the setting. Owed: checklist §242 (the OPPO against throwaways of two releases, and two computers a release apart).
  - **Review round** (approve once I1 is fixed; no Critical, one Important, four Nits). I1: a kept release was never forgotten, so a computer or phone moved back to a release before this one kept showing the old release, with a wrong "update this app". Now every identity replaces it: one that says none, or no version once cleaned, clears it in the trust store, in `computers.json` (on the link's identity) and on the phone, where the bridge now tells an identity with none from none heard and `HostStore` drops its record. N1: three doc comments put back on their own items. N2: Other computers shows the newer of the two words on a computer's release (the link it dials, and its own connections here). N3: both sides cut a claimed release at ASCII white space only; a no-break space makes it no release on both. N4: `protocol.md` says the release is self-reported. Commit 80fae019's message names the checklist section §240; it is §242.
- 2026-10-31: **PC9a: Linux views Windows, verified view-only.** The first live run of the PC-to-PC window in its untested direction: a throwaway server on the Arch laptop, paired one way with a throwaway on the Windows laptop whose trust entry kept Input, Audio, Power and the rest off, read Windows' real 2560x1440 screen through a stand-in for the window that kept counts only (no picture saved, no input, no sound). **Numbers:** by the home road the first frame came 2.0 to 2.3 s after the ask ("showing" at 0.5 s; most of the rest is Windows' per-session encoder probe and NVENC start, a lead for later), dial 7 to 30 ms, 16 to 17 frames a second on a mostly still screen at 0.8 to 1.3 Mbit/s, round trip 5 to 11 ms; by the internet road direct, first frame 2.0 s, dial 12 ms. The laptop's decoder (`view_live`'s new `the_decoder_on_a_running_pairs_view`, 10 s a way): software NV12 p50 6.8 ms p95 10.7 ms, VA-API on the Intel node 9.8/12.0 ms, auto 9.1/11.0 ms, software RGBA 10.5/21.6 ms; the NVIDIA node gave no picture. The render nodes had swapped since AGENTS.md's note (renderD128 is Intel on this boot); the note, as PR #577 left it, says never to assume a node. **Recovery:** the laptop's server paused 5 s and 30 s kept its session (frames back at once); `pocketdesk kick` on Windows ended the view with its reason and no redial; Windows' `pocketdesk devices` listed the laptop in a session. View-only focus moved neither clipboard; dropped test files of 300 kB and 5 MB landed whole (same SHA-256), and with Files off were refused by name. **Fixed here:** (1) a stopping server dropped its QUIC and internet endpoints without a close, so a window showing it kept a still picture with no "Reconnecting" for up to QUIC's 45 s idle timeout (33 s seen); it now closes each live session with "server stopping" on its own road (iroh's endpoint close carries no reason), then both endpoints, before the shared-input stop and on any return, and the window said Reconnecting (1) at once and had the picture back 3.2 s after the stop by the home road and 4.4 s by the internet road; (2) a view begun while the link was still coming up never listed the other computer's screens; they are asked again each 5 s until an answer, six times at most, one send in flight. The review's round also made only the server's own refusal final (a closed endpoint's transport "refused" during a stop is dialled again) and gave `view_live` a StopView guard. Not done: the desktop app's own window (it would show the owner's screen on the laptop, and its Stats are only readable from the picture), Control, Quality and sound. Windows gates and the laptop's Linux clippy and tests green.

- 2026-10-31: **The Linux tray in a build with the server.** Found by the laptop's owed-check run (§121): a build that compiles the server and the app together (`cargo build --workspace`, or both packages in one command) unifies zbus's `tokio` feature into the app, and ksni, which runs zbus on async-io, then connected from a thread with no Tokio runtime: `pocketdesk-tray` panicked ("there is no reactor running") and ran without an icon. The release, AUR and Debian builds compile the app apart (N10) and were not affected. `tray_linux.rs` now starts ksni with a small Tokio runtime entered, one worker thread running zbus's tasks while ksni waits on them, kept as long as the tray; with zbus on async-io it idles. A unit test runs work on that thread, which blocks on a task it spawned (the deadlock a runtime with no worker gave) and spawns another that runs after it returns. On the laptop, in the unified build: the item registers with waybar's watcher, no panic, and its `Activate` (a click) opens the window. Still open: in that build AccessKit's own zbus thread panics the same way at the app's start (N10's reason for the separate builds). `docs/AGENTS.md`'s VAAPI/NVENC gotcha now says the Intel and NVIDIA render nodes swap with drivers and boots, and NVENC needs NVIDIA's proprietary driver.
- 2026-10-30: **Remove a lost device from another of yours (remote revoke).** Asked on Reddit: can a paired phone be revoked remotely if it is lost or stolen, or does that need the server side? It needed the computer (its Devices page Unpair, or `pocketdesk unpair`). Now an owner device lists a computer's paired devices and removes one there. **Who may:** a phone paired with the computer's own code (marked `owners` in `trusted_devices.json` as it pairs; never someone else's, a profile's or a guest's, and a phone paired before this is not one until it pairs again), or another of the owner's computers paired both ways (PC1) whose grant neither waits for the link nor has lapsed; `pocketdesk devices --json` says `owner`. **What it does:** `kdeconnect.pocketdesk.remote_devices` (`list`, or `remove` with a `deviceId`, each with the asker's request id) answered by `remote_devices_answer`, the list naming each device's id, name, kind, whether it is an owner device, a guest or connected, and which is the asker (no key, address or permission). A removal is decided again under the trust store's lock: never the asker, never a device not paired, never the last owner device; then its trust, name, permissions, keys and a phone's contacts go, its sessions end and its control connections close at once (a new close broadcast the connection loop listens to, `not_paired` said first), a computer paired both ways is forgotten by the links' loop as Devices Unpair forgets it (Forgotten, tell_forget, drop_nudge), the computer notifies "<asker> removed <device> from this computer" and writes `device_removed` (with `by`). At most 5 removals a minute per asker. The same gate holds on a phone's connection, a computer's connection here and the link this computer keeps to the asker (`peer_computers::take`). **Where:** the desktop's Settings → Other computers → a computer paired both ways → "Devices there…" (asked through `computers_out/`, answered into `computers.json`'s `there`, taken only from the computer asked for the request waiting), each device with Remove… asked by name; the phone's computer card → "Devices on this computer" (`DevicesThereSheet`, `DevicesThereRule`, bridge `remote_devices.rs`). SECURITY limit 71 says the new power (a stolen owner phone can remove the owner's other devices there; the notification, the event and the computer's own re-pairing are the mitigations), and "Using it safely today" says to remove a lost device at once. **The last-owner rule:** as ruled, a removal never leaves the computer with no owner device; since the asker must itself still be an owner device as the store is written, this can bite only when two owner devices remove each other at once, and then the second finds itself removed and is refused. Tests: the gate (owner phone and both-ways computer yes; someone else's, a guest, a pending, a lapsed and a one-way computer, a stranger no), the owner mark through re-pairing and unpairing, the refusals (yourself, not paired, the mutual-removal race, the last-owner count), the rate limit, keys cleared, the words of the notification, the list carrying no secret, a forged ask from a non-owner refused through the plugin with nothing changed, a removal closing the device's connections and its sessions judged unpaired, the asking side taking only the awaited answer, the desktop's list and its words (kittest), the Activity line, the phone's rule (JVM). Not run here (daytime rules: no live runs, no NDK, no phone): everything on devices, owed in the checklist's §241, the bridge .so with the new JNI calls first. **Review round (PR #574):** I1, "Let my devices remove others from afar" (`remote_revoke`, on by default) in Settings, Other computers: off, a list or a removal is refused as `off`, and SECURITY 71 says a stolen owner device is removed at the computer (Devices, Unpair, or `pocketdesk unpair`); I2, the phone makes each ask's request id and takes only the answer naming it, in the bridge (`reply_answers`) and the sheet; I3, a test pairs through `handle_pair` with the owner's code, a guest grant, someone else's code, a profile's code with the profile gone, and a PIN shown while the code is someone else's, and only the first marks an owner phone; I4, a test runs the control loop over a duplex stream and sees the removal close it saying `not_paired`, and core's `kick_device` is tested to close that device's sessions alone. Nits: the last-owner test says what it checks (N1); a link this computer keeps counts as connected (N2); the notification names the asker by this computer's name for it, its kind and the start of its id (N3); the computers file and then the trust store are held through the decision, and keys and contacts go inside it (N4); the answer's catalogue row (N5); lists are paced at 30 a minute and SECURITY says the counts reset on a restart (N6); an answer of another kind, or about another device, is dropped, and a removal's wait ends with its 20 s (N7); the phone drops a row already gone, lists again after any removal and keeps no list after a refusal (N8); the connection listens from before it is seeded, and a lagged one checks its standing (N9); a store that does not read answers `failed` (N10); SECURITY says an older writer demotes owner phones (N11); closing "Devices there…" forgets the other's list (`close_there`, N12).
- 2026-10-30: **Keys by position (scancode mode) in a computer's screen window.** PC4 left it for later: the window typed text (layout-proof) and sent only the keys that type nothing as keys, so games and programs that read keys by where they sit got nothing for WASD. **The switch:** the bar's **Keys by position** (while the window controls that computer, off by default) sends every key, letters and digits too, as the evdev code of where it sits, from egui's `physical_key` through a full table (`view_input::BY_POSITION`: letters, digits, punctuation by place, F1 to F24, navigation, the modifiers both sides, the ISO 102nd key as `KEY_102ND`, the keypad's + as `KEY_KPPLUS`), with Shift going down at once like any key and the text this computer's layout made of a key dropped (that computer's layout types it). Text that came from no key still goes as text: an input method's commit, and a key egui places nowhere (egui reports no key at all for Caps Lock, Num Lock, Scroll Lock, Print Screen, Pause, Menu and the keypad's * and .; the keypad's digits, Enter, minus and slash come as their main-block twins, so they go as those: documented). Off, nothing changed. The choice is kept per computer in `computers.json` (`keys_by_position`, absent until switched), which only the server writes, so the bar asks it with a new `computers_out/` ask, `keys_by_position {device_id, on}`; `computer_view_scancodes` (off) in Settings → Other computers is the start for a computer never switched, read as each window opens; a window opened from another's bar takes its choice. **Held keys:** switching lets go of everything held there first (either way), and the window's existing releases (focus lost, View only, close, a rejoin's fresh input, reconnecting) cover keys held by position; a new input after a rejoin takes the mode again on its next pass. **The offer (PC7):** the first time the pointer is locked while keys go as text, the bar says "Games usually want Keys by position" with Turn on and Not now; once a window, kept after the lock is freed (the bar takes no clicks while locked), never switched on but by its button. **Hosts:** Windows already injected keys by scancode (`KEYEVENTF_SCANCODE`); the keypad's Enter and slash (E0 1C, E0 35) and the Japanese and Brazilian keys had no stroke and now do, tested by reading every scancode back through `windows_scan` and pressing it as itself. Linux uinput and the RemoteDesktop portal press the evdev code; the wlroots virtual keyboard presses it through its own US keymap (same key, US keysym: documented). The X11 rung (enigo) looked a printing key's US character up in the desktop's layout, so on any other layout it pressed another key: it now presses letters, digits, punctuation and keys enigo has no name for as their X keycode (evdev + 8, `XTestFakeKeyEvent` via `raw`), the named keys as before. That changes a phone's key presses on a non-US X11 desktop to positional too, as uinput and Windows already were. Tests: the table (every `egui::Key` placed once or unplaced on purpose, no place twice, each place a Windows scancode), keys and text by position and not, IME and unplaced text, the switch letting go; a test window driving the bar's switch (the ask written), a W held let go as the switch goes off, View only, focus loss and close (the relay a local socket); the offer's steps and its buttons with the pointer locked and freed; the setting's default, save and Settings row; the per-computer field and ask; the server keeping it; the X keycode rule; the scancode round trip. Nothing pressed a key on this computer. Owed: everything live, in the checklist's §240. Docs: the guide and protocol. **Review round (PR #573):** I1, the window's own Ctrl+Alt+Shift chords sent Shift (and a layout switch's Alt+Shift went whole) by position: of Ctrl, Alt and Shift a second kind now waits for a key, a click or the wheel, so the far side sees only the first; I2, a key that types nothing left its text-drop armed, which could swallow a later keypad `*`: armed only for keys that type, and cleared at each pass's end; I3, the X11 change is kept (every host now presses by place, shortcuts too: a phone's Ctrl+A on an AZERTY X11 desktop is Ctrl+Q), documented in the guide and protocol; I4, by position Ctrl or Shift with C, X, V, Insert or Delete went as taps at release (egui makes them copy, cut and paste): they now go down as egui says, held until the key is let go, at QWERTY's places (documented); N1, the input-method arm is gone (the picture asks for no IME); N2, the keypad twins are in the hover and Settings too; N3, text from no key leaves Shift held by position; N5, a failed save is logged and noted; N6, the added scancodes are also held to Microsoft's documented values, and the JIS and ABNT keys are a device check; N7, keys are let go as the view starts reconnecting (input stops while it does), tested in a window. N4 (modifiers held across a release are not pressed again) is left, documented. **Re-review:** C-A, by position a copy, cut or paste from a key not at QWERTY's place (Dvorak's C is QWERTY's J) left C held there: the held key is now let go with any key's release that is not held there, tested; I-A, holding back any second modifier hid a game's crouch-then-run: only the third of Ctrl, Alt and Shift waits now (the window's own chord), two kinds go at once (so a far side's Alt+Shift layout switch can be pressed, documented), tested both ways; N5, the bar says Not saved when this computer's server is not running. **Re-review 2:** C-B, one held copy key at a time meant a Ctrl+C, V roll left C held there: the copy, cut and paste keys held are a set, each let go with its own key, all of them with a key not held there (Dvorak) or as Ctrl or Shift is let go; tested on QWERTY and Dvorak rolls and with Ctrl let go first. N-B (a held-back third modifier waits for a key even after the chord drops) is left.

this item fixes the Important ones and the Minors. **I1, the window's mic:** a computer paired both ways now has "Use the phone as a microphone" on (`computer_mine()`), one paired one way keeps it off (a computer paired both ways before keeps its stored off); a focus loss waits 0.3 s before its ask, and a window of the same computer taking the focus then is a hand-over that asks and pushes nothing; from one computer's window to another's, the push waits for the first's answer, up to 1.5 s (and 0.25 s for it to land); 62 lists every packet the relay passes; 69 says a grab on a dead link ends in about 30 s; 65 and 69 say I2's and I3's rules. **Minors:** unpair on the Devices page ends the view, tells the other to forget the wake facts and drops the nudge (M1); - 2026-10-30: **PC7: pointer lock and relative mouse for games and 3D.** The seventh item of the PC-to-PC plan (gap 7: the window sent absolute moves only, and the relay refused `MouseMove {dx,dy}`; gap 12: the window and shared input did not know each other). **Lock the pointer:** while a computer's screen window controls that computer, its bar has **Lock the pointer**, and Ctrl+Alt+Shift+M (kept here, as the stats' chord) switches it: `ViewportCommand::CursorGrab(Locked)` where winit can (Wayland), else `Confined` (Windows, X11) with the pointer put back in the picture's middle whenever it leaves the middle half; either way the pointer is first put in the picture's middle and hidden each pass (`CursorIcon::None`, which egui-winit keeps hidden). "Ctrl+Alt+Shift+M frees the pointer" shows over the picture for 3 s. It is freed by the same chord, by Ctrl+Alt+Shift pressed and let go alone (PC4's release chord, read from egui's keys and modifiers so it works without PC4's hook and on Linux), and as the window loses the focus (only a focus the system said), closes, turns View only, stops controlling, ends or reconnects; a focus that comes back does not lock again. **Relative motion:** while locked, egui's raw `MouseMoved` deltas are summed over a pass and go as `MouseMove {dx,dy}` in whole steps, the sub-pixel remainder kept for the next pass (and forgotten on a release), split past `i16`, a wild pass capped at four packets and a non-number dropped; buttons go without a position, after the movement before them. The window tells its server with a new view-relay message (`0x15`, one byte: locked or freed), and the relay's reader for that window's connection keeps the flag: `input_allowed(packet, pointer_locked)` passes `MouseMove` only while the window's last word on that connection is "locked" (a new connection starts freed; the window says it again after a rejoin). **Shared input pauses its edges** (gap 12): the reader takes a `shared_input::view_hold::PointerHold` (a count, so a replaced window winding down never clears a newer one's) while locked, dropped as the window frees it or the connection ends. The driver's loop reads it each pass beside `receive::driven_by`, as PC4's grab reads where the shared keyboard is the other way: `Driver::set_view_held` takes the hooks out on Windows and the barriers off on Linux (`Mode::Off`), leads no edge anywhere, refuses the switch key with a note, brings a visit under way home first, says so once on the badge (nothing while a game or being driven already holds the edges), and leads them on again as the hold ends. **Auto-suggest:** out of scope: the host does not report its cursor's visibility (no cursor field in the stream or the view's status), so nothing cheap to suggest from. No setting was added: the chord is fixed, as the stats' is, and the lock is per window. Tests: the relay message both ways and its refusals; the gate with and without the lock; a loopback reader test (moves before the lock and after the release dropped, a bad byte ending the reader); the hold's count; the driver paused, the switch key refused, a visit brought home, quiet under a game, and the edges back after; the window's lock decision (switch, focus, control, picture, not again by itself), the release chord over several passes and in one, spoiled by another key or the switch's M; the hint's time; the grab mode for each system; the re-centring; the viewport commands; the accumulation with remainders, the buttons without a position, the split and the cap; and when the relay is told again. Not run here: the grab itself (an ignored test, `the_real_grab_locks_and_frees`, behind `POCKETDESK_POINTER_TEST_MACHINE=1`, never on the owner's box). Owed: everything live, in the checklist's §235. Docs: the guide, protocol, local API and SECURITY (limit 62). **Review round (PR #563):** a failed write to the relay now ends the connection (it left it half open, the relay never hearing the pointer freed); the lock is a lease, said again each second and lapsing 3 s after the last, and a focus lost frees it at the relay too, so a hung window never keeps the edges paused; the bar takes no clicks while locked (on Wayland the hidden pointer stays on its switch, and the first click in a game unlocked it); the grab asks Confined before Locked and re-centres whatever the mode, the mode read by winit's own rule (`WAYLAND_DISPLAY`, else `WAYLAND_SOCKET`); a relative move the relay's full queue dropped is owed to the next; a picture-size change while locked keeps the input; the hold's notes are one line, quiet where the badge cannot be drawn, and silent while the session here is locked; the release test asserts a closed socket again with an explicit shutdown (mio 1.2.0's Windows sockets are inheritable, so a parallel test's child kept a dropped one open), and a test window drives the lock, a click on the greyed bar, the chord's free and the close's free end to end. Ctrl+Alt+Shift alone also gives back PC4's keys (documented). Merged main (PC5 took §234; this is §235).

- 2026-10-30: **PC5: a faster picture, with hardware decode, a lighter pipe and HEVC.** The fifth item of the PC-to-PC plan (gaps 5 and 6: software decode through an RGBA pipe, and H.264 only). **Measured first:** the stats overlay's Decode line says the median and 95th percentile of the last second's decode times (each picture against its access unit) and the way it decodes, and a new Pipe line the bytes each picture brings out of the sidecar. **Hardware decode:** the window's ffmpeg sidecar tries `-hwaccel d3d11va` on Windows and `-hwaccel vaapi` on Linux at each render node in the encoder's order (or only `POCKETDESK_VAAPI_DEVICE`), both with the card's own frames downloaded (`-hwaccel_output_format` and `hwdownload`), so a card that cannot decode gives no picture (ffmpeg stops, or goes on giving nothing) rather than decoding on the processor unsaid, and the 2 s rule moves on; then `-hwaccel auto` (its log says which it took); then software. A way that ends, or gives no picture 2 s after its first access unit, is left for the next from a keyframe; the way that gave a picture holds for the view, a rejoin too. `computer_view_hw_decode` (on, Settings → Other computers) leaves the card out. **NV12:** pictures come out as NV12, 12 bits a pixel against RGBA's 32, and a small glow shader in an egui paint callback samples the luma and chroma planes (R8 and RG8 textures, rows unpadded, odd sizes rounded up as ffmpeg writes them) and turns them to RGB by the matrix the stream says, read from ffmpeg's output line: unsignalled is BT.601 limited, which is what the encoders here make (ffmpeg's BGRA-to-YUV conversion signals nothing) and how ffmpeg's own RGBA read it before, so colours are unchanged; BT.709 and full range when the stream says so. The ruling asked for BT.709 limited "matching the encoder": the encoder emits BT.601, so the shader follows the stream. The app tells the window whether it draws with glow; with another renderer (the snapshots' wgpu) or a shader that fails, frames come as RGBA as before, and a picture already NV12 is converted on the processor, as a screenshot is. **HEVC:** the desktop app, whose sidecar decodes, decodes a 91-byte HEVC sample (libx265, 16x16 grey) with its own `ffmpeg` once as it starts (not as the first window opens, whose ask would come first; an ask before the probe has answered waits up to 1.5 s for it, off the UI thread), and its view ask says `hevc` with that good and `computer_view_hevc` (on, "Use HEVC when both can") and `computer_view_hw_decode` on (on the processor HEVC is slower than H.264); the server offers `CODEC_CAP_HEVC` only on that ask and its own settings, so an app before PC5 never hears HEVC, and a view whose last way gives no HEVC picture is asked for again without it, once; the other computer sends HEVC with its `allow_hevc` on. A keyframe that carries an HEVC VPS (`0x40 0x01`, which no H.264 unit begins with) sends the window a new view message, `6` (no payload), again when its parameter sets change; the sidecar then starts with no size and takes it from ffmpeg's output line, said once the first picture is decoded and before it is written. The stats name the codec. **Numbers** (this Windows box, RTX 3060 laptop, two throwaway servers on loopback, view-only, a debug build, the window's decoder fed by the relay for 10 s a way, `view_live`; pictures never kept): H.264 test pattern 1080p60, 600 frames in 10 s each way: software RGBA (before) decode p50 9.1 ms, p95 10.1 ms, 8.3 MB a picture; software NV12 4.9/5.3 ms, 3.1 MB; d3d11va NV12 4.8/5.2 ms; auto 5.2/5.5 ms. HEVC test pattern: software RGBA 12.6/14.1 ms; software NV12 7.8/8.4 ms; d3d11va NV12 3.9/4.3 ms; auto 4.2/4.6 ms. This screen, 2560x1440, about 140 frames in 10 s: H.264 RGBA 16.0/18.9 ms and 14.7 MB a picture, NV12 6.1/8.7 ms and 5.5 MB, d3d11va 8.1/9.9 ms; HEVC RGBA 20.2/24.7 ms, NV12 10.9/12.7 ms, d3d11va 6.7/9.0 ms. The pipe is 2.7 times lighter everywhere; NV12 alone about halves the decode time; the card wins most for HEVC, and for H.264 at 1440p software NV12 was quicker here (the card's gain there is the processor's time, not measured). The HEVC frames of this mostly still screen were not smaller than H.264's at the same bitrate setting (9 KB against 4-5 KB a frame); its bitrate gain on a moving picture is owed. Tests: the plane maths, colour matrices (and NV12 from the sidecar against its own RGBA, mean step under 3), each way's arguments, ffmpeg's lines, the ways' order on each system, the give-up rule, the planes' fallback, the size from the first picture, a card decoding or stopping, HEVC's shape, caps and probe, the view message, the settings; the shader in a real glow window drawing a known NV12 picture (ignored, run here: colours and orientation). Not run: the hook itself (an ignored test, never on the owner's box). Owed: the menu both ways, the grab, the release, focus loss and shared input with the Linux laptop, in the checklist's §231. A closed connection now frees it, an ask has 10 s to reveal, and at most 3 prompts show in 10 minutes from all addresses. I2: the other's address list was pinned. (2) The owner's pasted pairing link began with a byte-order mark, so Pair stayed grey: `pair_link::clean_link` drops whitespace, the mark and zero-width characters at both ends, used by `PairLink::from_uri`, the desktop app's check and what it sends, and the phone's `PairLink.parse`. - 2026-10-29: **PC2: the window says how it's doing, and comes back by itself.** The second item of the PC-to-PC plan, on BK7's window that shows another computer's screen. **Stats:** a Stats switch on the window's bar (off by default, remembered as `computer_view_stats`, also in Settings → Other computers) shows over the picture the road (home network, internet direct or by a relay) and how long it took to reach, the picture's size and codec, the frames a second that came and were shown, the bitrate, the round trip, the decoder's time and the frames lost, late and dropped. The server counts what comes (core's `missed_frames_in_window` for the gaps) and sends it once a second in the view's status (`stats`, with `reconnecting`, every field with a serde default so either side may be older); the window times its own decoder, each picture against the access unit it came from. **Reconnect:** a view that was shown and then dropped is dialled again by the server, after 1, 2, 4 and 8 s, then every 15 s, for about two minutes, while the window dims the last picture and says "Reconnecting… (n)" with Cancel; the relay keeps the window joined, so the picture comes back in the same view, and the window's last quality and view-only are said again to the new session. An end the other computer chose (a close reason with kicked, refused, not paired or not allowed) is not dialled again and says so ("Desk ended the view (kicked by user)."); a view shown again for 30 s starts a later drop's tries afresh, so a computer that drops it each time is not dialled for ever; Stop, Cancel or a closed window abort the view's task, so nothing dials after them. BK7's limit of three rejoins in ten seconds, for the window's own hop to the relay, is unchanged. **A faster first picture:** BK7 dialled the home network first, 3 s an address, then the internet path, so a computer away from home showed its first frame 3.8 to 4.7 s after the ask. Now every road is dialled at once: a home address wins as soon as it comes up, the internet path after waiting 300 ms for a home address still trying; the race is over the transports alone (`Race`, a pure state machine, driven by a `JoinSet`), and the losers are cancelled or closed before this computer names itself on them, so the other computer starts one session. Tests: red first for the status fields, the frame counts, the backoff and the two-minute stop, the reasons not dialled again, the replayed choices, the race (state machine and tasks), the overlay's lines, the decoder's clock, the reconnecting words and Cancel; a loopback test whose stand-in hangs up as its server stops and takes the next session (the same view comes back, a Stop while it waits never dials again); the live two-server test on the test pattern, view-only: both roads at once, the home network won (dial 11 ms, first frame 1.09 s), one session on the other computer, then the internet path alone (dial 8 ms, first frame 0.99 s). Owed: the stats, the first picture, a restart, a Wi-Fi drop and Cancel with the Linux laptop, in the checklist's §224. Docs: the guide, local API and protocol.

The link is not signed, so the flag only asks: the computer that made it pairs both ways only when its own PIN file says so, the pasting computer asked (`mine: true` with `facts`, what to list it by, in its `kdeconnect.pair`), and the connection proved a certificate; adding the word to an ordinary link, or taking it off, pairs one way as before. - 2026-10-29: **A computer's answers follow its new link at once.** Found in BK11's runs: after the driving computer's server restarted, the computer driven sent its answers (screens, refusals, share-test's timings) on the connection from before the restart for about 50 s. `receive.rs`'s `send_to` kept a `Channel` per computer, which holds the connection it was opened on; `link::register` replaced the link but not the cached channel, and the old connection, its other end gone without a word, lived on until its 45 s idle timeout, every send into it "succeeding". `Channel::is_current` says whether a channel's connection is still the link registered for its computer, `Channel::send` refuses one that is not, and `send_to` opens the channel anew on the new link; the drivers (Windows' hooks and the input-capture portal) do the same in their `channel()`, greeting the computer again, and mid-visit their next send fails and the keyboard and mouse come home. Tests, red first: an answer after the link is replaced arrives on the new link at once (it timed out before), and a channel on a replaced or gone link is not current and refuses to send. Not checked live: the Arch laptop was offline; checklist §222 owes it.
- 2026-10-29: **BK11: no pacing stall on the computer link.** BK10 found a key after a pause still waited about 25 ms on the link between computers: BBR (noq's Bbr3, every iroh connection's controller) paces a quiet link at the low rate it has seen, so a key that followed a ping waited for the pacer. The link now has its own ALPN, `pocketdesk-computer/1`, on Cubic. The dialing computer asks for it through `connect_with_opts` with `pocketdesk-control/1` offered beside it, so a computer from before still answers, and sends on Cubic whichever the other picks (`computer_link_transport`: the endpoint's limits and timeouts, Cubic whatever `POCKETDESK_CC` says). The WAN endpoint reads the offered ALPNs from the dialer's first packet (`Incoming::decrypt().alpns()`, best effort) and accepts that one with `accept_with` on a Cubic server config that prefers it; anything else, or a first packet it cannot read, is accepted as before, and the endpoint's own list puts the control ALPN first, so such a dialer gets that one on BBR. Both ALPNs go to the same control loop and the same checks; the new one grants nothing (protocol.md, protocol-spec.md, SECURITY.md). Phones, BK7's view and the home network's QUIC stay on BBR. development-setup.md said `POCKETDESK_CC` defaulted to new_reno; it has been BBR since BG4. Unit tests: the ALPN choice and each choice's controller, the endpoint's ALPN order, and four loopback pairs of real iroh endpoints, new to new, new to older, older to new and a link accepted unread, asserting the negotiated ALPN and each end's controller (`congestion_state(PathId::ZERO)`, downcast). Measured as BK10 was, Windows (Ethernet) to the Arch laptop (Wi-Fi) between throwaway servers, Windows on main's binary then this branch's: a key after a 1 s pause, the network's one way p95 22.5 to 27.6 ms before (median 23.8, 7 runs) and 2.2 to 3.3 ms after (median 2.6, 6 runs). The burst's p50 went from 2.5 to 4.8 ms to 0.6 to 1.6 ms; its p95 was 2.2 to 3.5 ms in 6 of 12 runs after and 24 to 73 ms in the rest, mostly in a lossy spell in which some runs lost up to 15 pings in 202, where BBR's runs then had no loss but three stalls of 112 to 483 ms. Cubic sends about a packet a message, three times BBR's. In-link pings every 40 ms (a local build) no longer cost a paused key anything (p95 2.2 to 2.8 ms), so BK10's byte outside the link could give way to them later; it stays for now. Found on the way: after the driving computer's server restarted, the computer driven sent its answers on the old connection for about 50 s (timings lost, with either binary), likely `receive.rs`'s cached channel; owed. Checklist §222.

- 2026-10-29: **BK10: no lag while sharing.** The latency run had Pocketdesk's own share of a key under 0.5 ms but the network's about 16 ms one way, from Windows (Ethernet) to the Arch laptop (Wi-Fi). Measured first: turning the laptop's Wi-Fi power saving off did not move that 16 ms (15.9 and 15.7 ms), though it did take idle pings to the laptop from p50 54 to 65 ms down to 1.2 ms. The 16 ms was Windows' 15.6 ms timer tick: the messages left Windows in bursts 15.3 ms apart while the network's own one way was 0.35 ms. "Keep the connection quick while sharing" (`shared_input_low_latency`, on; config, and the desktop app's Keyboard and mouse section on both systems) now, while this computer's keyboard and mouse are on another computer or a phone, while another computer's cursor is on this one, and while `share-test` runs, (1) on Windows holds the server's timer at 1 ms (`timeBeginPeriod`, and Windows 11's power throttling told not to ignore it; holds counted across the driver, the receiver and share-test, given back after the last), and (2) on any system sends the other computer a byte every 50 ms at the address and port its link reaches it at, on this computer's own network only (after the review: first to its discard port), so this computer's Wi-Fi never dozes between keys. Not held merely while sharing is on: a finer timer wakes the whole computer more often, and both cost a laptop a little battery. Measured with one binary, the setting off then on: `share-test --keys 500 --moves 500` network p50 17.8 and 16.5 ms off, 2.7 and 3.1 ms on (p95 18.1 off, 5.8 and 7.2 on); a byte every 50 or 80 ms took the laptop's idle pings from p50 69 ms to 2.3 ms. The brief's way for the Wi-Fi, NetworkManager's `Device.Reapply` with `802-11-wireless.powersave = 2`, cannot work: polkit refused it from an ssh session, and NetworkManager 1.58 refuses it anyway (its Wi-Fi device reapplies only the MTU, the seen BSSIDs and wake-on-WLAN); `iw` needs root. Pinging the computer driven every 40 ms instead (built first, then replaced) kept the Wi-Fi awake but made a key after a pause later (p50 25.8 ms): the link's BBR paces a quiet link at the rate it sees, so a key beside a ping waited about 25 ms for the pacer, which a capture at the laptop's card showed leaving Windows that late. Hence the byte goes outside the link. That pacing is still the tail of a key after a pause (p95 27 to 33 ms either way); with `POCKETDESK_CC=cubic` on Windows it was p95 2.6 ms, so a congestion controller for the links between computers is the next step. Also: `share-test --pause <ms>` spaces the events and pings meanwhile as a driver does, and each key is lined up by the pings near it (two wall clocks drift by milliseconds over a minute). Checklist §215 corrected (the 16 ms was the tick, and its "first key after a minute" item named a feature that did not exist) and §221 added. Gates: Windows' workspace tests 2139 passed, with one control-vector test (a 4 MiB line closing the connection in time) failing once under load and passing alone and in a rerun of the server's 978; on the laptop the workspace clippy clean and `cargo test -p pocketdesk-server` 973 passed, the owner's state untouched. - 2026-10-29: **Linux fixes from the laptop run.** A real run on an Arch laptop (FFmpeg 9, no `hostname`) found three bugs the Windows gates could not. (1) FFmpeg 9 removed `-vsync`, so the desktop app's decoder sidecar exited at once and neither the phone's cast window nor another computer's window (BK7) showed a picture; the server's encoder sidecar had the opposite gap, `-fps_mode` that FFmpeg 4.4 lacks. `streaming::ffmpeg_flags` asks each ffmpeg once through `-h long` and both sidecars use the flag it lists. (3) A workspace test run created the owner's `~/.local/state/pocketdesk/trusted_devices.json`: 38 server unit tests and 4 integration tests reached the state or config folder before any test had set the once-per-process throwaways. `paths::use_test_dirs` sets both and each of those tests calls it; an audit that logged every resolution and ran each resolving test alone found none reaching a real folder afterwards on Windows or Linux, and the core's own tests now panic if they do. Laptop: desktop 296 passed, server 916 passed, workspace clippy clean, the state folder unchanged; Windows: workspace 2084 passed.
- 2026-10-29: **BK8: Linux drives too.** The shared keyboard and mouse leave a Linux screen as they leave a Windows one, through the desktop's input-capture portal (`org.freedesktop.portal.InputCapture`: GNOME 45+, KDE Plasma 6.1+, Hyprland with xdg-desktop-portal-hyprland 1.4+) and its libei stream, received in pure Rust by reis (MIT; ashpd 0.13.10's `input_capture` feature, no bump: it has `CreateSession2`, `Start` and the restore token). The same pure `Driver` runs it, with three new inputs: `Captured::EvKey` (libei's evdev codes, no scancode round trip), `Captured::Edge` (a barrier's push: where and which way, crossing as such a push off that edge would, with no watched position before it; chosen over synthesizing two `Moved`s, which the reseed after a mode change would swallow) and `Captured::CaptureLost` (the desktop ended the capture); every Windows drive test passes untouched. `barriers` turns this computer's screens in its layout (the portal's zones) into one barrier per run of edge pixels crossing onto the same reachable computer, as `Layout::crossing` has it, on the zones' outer boundary (bottom and right lines one past the zone), and an activation back into an `Edge`; its `FlipBreaker` walls an edge after three activations in a second (xdph #435, fractional scale), said once. `keys_evdev` counts the modifiers from key events (xdph #436 and GNOME never say them), spots the switch key and forgets what was down at each switch. `capture_restore` keeps the portal's restore token owner-only (`input_capture.token`, never logged; the dialog shows once). `portal_glue` is the seam: what the portal says becomes what the driver takes and what it wants becomes `Backend` asks (arm the barriers, disarm, release with a position); a hit that leads nowhere is let go of at once inside the edge; away, relative motion is a move from the park with its fractions kept, buttons, the wheel (discrete first) and keys go on; home releases where the cursor lands; Deactivated, Disabled or a closed stream bring the keyboard and mouse home with key-ups first; a disabled capture is enabled again only at the next layout pass. `portal_drive` is the Linux loop and supervisor (links, phones, pings, sound, greetings, refusals as Windows' loop; the zones are this computer's screens; Hyprland's full-screen window holds the edges for the gaming lock; a refused dialog is not asked again until sharing is toggled), written over any backend so Windows builds and lints it too. `capture_linux` is the real backend: ashpd's session on a thread of its own (reis's stream is not `Send`), barriers set on a disabled session (GNOME's portal wants that) and set again for each new zone set. The Keyboard and mouse page says how it stands on Linux (ready, needs permission, unsupported, fractional scale, also warned of before an edge flips), no longer that driving from Linux comes later. Out of scope, as briefed: the layer-shell, X11 and evdev backends, and Sway; starting a crossing by key at home (the portal gives no keys there); files dragged across stay Windows'. Tests: the pure parts on every system, and a fake-EIS test (reis's EIS side on a socket pair) carrying pointer, button, scroll and key events through the receiver and the glue to the messages Windows gets. Gates: Windows fmt, both clippy profiles and the workspace tests (2108 passed); WSL (Arch) fmt, both clippy profiles and the server and core tests (1778 passed, the fake-EIS and owner-only token tests among them); the Linux backend was also type-checked and linted for the Linux target from Windows while WSL was down.   - **First review round** (spec met; one Critical, two Important, thirteen Minor). C1: the capture thread made ashpd's process-wide D-Bus connection on its own runtime, so its end would have left every later portal call (the phone's screen sharing, the next capture) waiting forever; it now opens a connection of its own, and the session task runs under catch_unwind so a panic still disables and closes the session (a stand-in Calls test). - 2026-10-29: **BK6: sound follows the mouse.** The third extra after the shared keyboard and mouse. With "Hear the computer you're driving here" (`shared_input_sound`, off; in the desktop app's Keyboard and mouse section, on Windows) the Windows driver asks the computer its cursor arrives on for its sound (`SoundWanted`, tag 16, on the stream after each arrival; never a phone) and plays it while the cursor is there, letting it go as the cursor leaves, moves on or comes home, or the setting goes off. The computer driven takes the ask only from the computer driving it now, over the direct link that arrival needed (whoever may drive a computer may hear it: SECURITY limit 61), for that visit only: its leave, a new arrival, a second's silence, its link or permission going end it. Only then does it capture its default output (PipeWire on Linux, WASAPI on Windows), cut it into 20 ms frames whatever the capture's quantum, encode Opus 48 kHz stereo at 96 kbit/s, and send each packet as a datagram (`Sound`, tag 18, at most 1000 bytes of Opus so it fits the smallest datagram), dropped rather than queued while more than 2 KB of datagrams wait; input goes the other way, so it never waits behind sound, nor does a ping's answer. A capture starts only once the one before has let go of the sound (its thread ended), so asks toggled fast never run two; one that cannot capture is told once (`SoundUnavailable`, tag 17), flashed on Windows. The driver plays through a jitter buffer (40 ms to start, at most 60 ms waiting with the oldest dropped, a late packet dropped) into the phone's player, now shared as `remote_sound::Player`, at most 60 ms at the output, and stops at once as it stops asking. Along the way: a stream message of a tag a build does not know is now skipped by its length instead of ending the stream (one unknown tag had cut off every key after it); and Linux's sound capture now offers PipeWire the 16-bit stereo format its callback reads, where it offered none (so the phone sessions' Linux sound, never checked, may not have worked). Two seams keep tests silent: `POCKETDESK_TEST_AUDIO_TONE` sends a tone instead of capturing, `POCKETDESK_COMPUTER_AUDIO_FILE` writes a WAV instead of playing. TDD: the unknown-tag skip, the receiver's ask and the visit count went red first; the sound module's tests (asking per visit, the start/stop state machine under rapid toggles with a capture slow to stop, the jitter buffer's drop policy, the framing, the datagram room) were written with it, and the toggle and the drop policy were checked to fail with their guard removed. A 1 kHz tone crossed a real link between two endpoints here into the WAV at its pitch and level; no live run between two servers, which would have moved this computer's cursor. Owed: the Linux build (CI) and checklist §218 at the desk, by ear. The review's first round found nothing critical or important. Of its nine minor findings, six were fixed. A sound packet now checks that the link is still direct before it goes, so a link turned relayed mid-visit carries none in the moment before the visit ends (M2). A computer's "no sound" reason is cut to 160 characters on one line before it is kept or flashed (M3). The capture starts on a blocking thread, since WASAPI waits up to 3 s for its device and on a Windows computer being driven that had held a runtime thread carrying input (M4). "No sound from" a computer flashes once a link and reason, not at every crossing (M5). The guide says to update both computers first, since a build from before BK6 brings the pointer home at the first key after each ask (M7). And the player sleeps until a packet comes when it has nothing to play (M9). Three are left. M1: the PipeWire format now applies to phone sessions' Linux sound too, owed by ear with checklist line 1695. M6: the computer driven shows no sign its sound is heard elsewhere; SECURITY limit 61 says so. M8: the driver's glue has no test of its own, so §218 covers it at the desk.
