- 2026-10-29: **BK4: files dragged across the edge.** The first extra after the shared keyboard and mouse. On Windows, a drag of files in Explorer carried across the edge and let go on the computer driven sends them there, with "Drag files across the edge" (`shared_input_files`, on; in the desktop app's Keyboard and mouse section, on Windows only). Each file or folder dropped is queued in the outbox as the desktop app's Send queues one, so the server's checks, a folder's send and the sent list follow, and they land where any file from this computer lands. Windows drops a drag where the cursor is, and while away the cursor is parked at the centre of the screen it left. The brief put a drop target in a strip along the facing edge with the cursor parked inside it, but the hook's points stop at the screen's edge, so a park there would cut short every move toward it, the main direction on the other computer. So the drop target (the catcher: 64 px, one step from see-through, on top, never activated, on an OLE thread of its own) goes under the parked cursor instead, put up before the cursor is parked there and only while a mouse button is held here (a drag carried along), and taken down before the cursor comes home, so a drag carried back drops on Windows' own. Files over it are taken as a copy; text or a window dragged across drops nothing, where before it dropped into whatever window lay under the park. Esc while files are over it is left to Windows, which cancels the drag, and is not sent on. The drop flashes "Sending 3 files to <computer>" here, and the sent list is watched for 30 s so a send that fails ("Files over the internet" off, Files not allowed there) says why. Logs give counts and bytes, never names. Unit-tested: the catcher's ups and downs, the drop's decision table, the words, Esc, and the sent list's outcome (RED on stubs, then GREEN); no test makes a window, registers a drop target or moves the cursor. Owed: the desk checks in §216, first of all whether Windows' drag finds the catcher under the parked cursor; a server running elevated cannot take Explorer's drop. The review's first round: a catcher slow to show nudges the cursor a pixel and back as injected input once it is up (the hooks pass it), so the drag looks again; a copy only where the drag's source allows one; the computer a drop goes to is kept by the catcher at each crossing and read at the drop itself, and a drag that came home first sends nothing and says so; only the first 100 paths are read; the catcher's thread starts as the setting turns on; and the sends to another computer no longer log a file's name or path, failed or retried. Linux untouched: it does not drive.
- 2026-10-29: **BK3: images follow the mouse.** Shared input's clipboard carried only text, because the path between computers carried only text. Now, when the clipboard holds no text, its image goes too (a PNG of up to 32 MB, known by its SHA-256 as N4's are), on the link's transfer streams as files go, so only with "Files over the internet" on on both. At a switch it is marked as following the mouse and taken under exactly the rule text is (`follow_taken`). To a computer linked as this one's client it is told (`{image: {id, size}, follows: true}`) and that computer fetches it on the link, served only for the image told at the last switch and until the next; to a computer this one keeps a link to it is offered (`clipboard_image` without a port) on a stream that computer opens. Nothing dials back. Never back to the computer it came from, never the same image to a computer twice in a row (until its clipboard holds something else), and never a copy marked private or from a program Settings keep here; logs say who and how large only. A switch's push runs on a task of its own, and a later switch, or the keyboard and mouse coming home, drops an image still on its way at either end; while one lands, nothing older goes back to its sender, so a quick crossing back cannot swap two clipboards. "Clipboard with my computers" sends images too, unmarked, without the phones' switch. An image counts as sent to a computer from the start of its send (a tell, for 10 s until the fetch comes), so a switch's push and the watcher's never send it twice; a failed send clears only its own note. `receive_bytes` presented its key even on a stream the other end had opened, which the loopback test caught. Unit-tested: what follows, the echo rules, the acceptance rule, the packets, and an image both ways over the real `hand_over` and transfer code in memory; the ignored `two_computers` test sends images both ways between two real servers on stand-in clipboards. Owed: the desk checks in §215 and §86.
- 2026-10-28: **BK2: what the final review of shared input found.** A review of the whole feature after its nine parts merged found eleven things, fixed in one pass. The driving side had no watchdog of its own: after a Wi-Fi drop of a second and a half, Linux let go of its silent driver while Windows stayed away, its cursor hidden and its keys swallowed, until QUIC gave up 45 s later. Away, the driver now pings the computer driven every 200 ms and comes home after a second with no answer, that computer's edge a wall until it answers again. A pong went to the channel opened to that computer last, so a refusal or an Identify, each on a channel of its own, took the driver's answers; each pong now goes to the channel whose ping it answers. The computer driven counted silence from when its loop got to a message, so a held-up loop could end a live session; it counts from when messages came off the link. A server stop or an uninstall while away never told the driver, leaving Windows' cursors blank and the other computer's keys held for a second: the stop now brings the keyboard and mouse home first (waiting at most a second), and a guard restores the cursors however the supervisor ends. Also: Win+L while away is never sent on: Windows handles it itself and locks this computer, and Pocketdesk, hearing the key or the session lock, brings the keyboard and mouse home (corrected 2026-10-30 in PR #559: the hook cannot keep Win+L from Windows); a hook that fails to go in while away brings them home; the computer driven refuses a cursor that arrives over a relay (a new refusal reason, 3); the Keyboard and mouse page shows names tamed and clipped; the status file is kept every half minute while sharing is off, not every second; an input thread left behind no longer clears a newer one's state; and the guide says to pair two computers one way. All unit-tested; nothing ran on a desktop, and the Linux laptop was offline, so the Linux gates are CI's. Owed at the desk (checklist §214): a pulled network bringing the keyboard and mouse home within about a second, and a server stopped while away.
- 2026-10-28: **BK: shared keyboard and mouse.** One keyboard and mouse across the owner's computers, Windows driving Linux for now (#517 to #528 and this one). The two computers' iroh link carries it: pointer moves, activity notes and pings as QUIC datagrams, everything else framed on a `PDSI` uni stream ahead of the control stream, and only while the link is direct. On Windows, pushing the pointer off a screen edge that faces another computer (or the switch key, Ctrl+Alt+Right) carries the cursor there: low-level hooks swallow the keys and mouse and send them, the Windows cursor parked and hidden, positions in logical pixels so mixed scales meet edge to edge. The computer driven injects them only from a paired computer with the new Keyboard and mouse permission (off until turned on), lets go of everything held when the cursor leaves, the link or permission goes, or the driver is silent for a second, refuses a computer it will not take (which then stops crossing onto it), and stays awake while driven. The gaming lock keeps the keyboard and mouse on Windows while a full-screen game or a listed program is in front, with no hook in at all; the switch key can force them off a game unless a kernel anti-cheat runs or the program in front is elevated. The desktop app's Keyboard and mouse page arranges the computers by dragging, shows each link and both permissions, and Identify flashes each computer's name; each switch flashes the name of the computer that has them, and the tray says where they are. Last (this entry's PR): the clipboard follows the mouse (`shared_input_clipboard`, on): the text copied here goes to the computer the cursor crosses onto, once per crossing, and the other computer's comes back as the cursor comes home, without "Clipboard with my computers" needing to be on (a marked push, taken only with both settings on there and from a computer it shares input with, which could therefore set that clipboard's text this way at any time, not only at a switch); text only, private copies and pairing links never. The driver's 200 ms activity note also keeps either laptop's Wi-Fi out of power saving between keys. `share-test` now measures latency: each key and move is timed from the script taking it to the link here, and from the link to the injector there (the computer driven sends its timings back when asked, `AskTimings`/`Timings`), the network's one way share lined up by the fastest ping's answer, which now carries the answerer's clock; the median and 95th percentile are printed, kept in `shared_input_latency.json` and shown on Diagnostics. Measured earlier, live on the Linux laptop (a headless Sway its own session never saw): 102 keys in order and none held, a killed sender's key let go about a second later, a lock screen typed into. Between two throwaway servers on this Windows box (loopback, the receiver only recording) the pings' round trip was 0.9 to 2.0 ms. The latency over the real Wi-Fi was not measured: the Linux laptop was offline for this run, so Pocketdesk's share against its 5 ms target (95th percentile) is owed, and so are the Linux gates for this PR (CI's Linux jobs decide them). Owed at the desk (checklist §214 and §215): Windows to Linux at the edge and by the key, typing, Win+L, a pulled network, mixed scales, the gaming lock with real games and anti-cheats, the badges, Identify, the tray, the clipboard both ways and a private copy staying put, the first key after a pause, Linux kept awake, the arrangement's crossings, and the latency target over the real Wi-Fi.
- 2026-10-28: **BJ, after 0.7.0: the installer, stopping and starting, and the AUR and F-Droid.** The 0.7.0 dry run failed on the installer's smoke test, which had never passed in CI: its uninstaller ran `pocketdesk stop`, gave up after 10 s, and could not delete `pocketdesk.exe` while the server, started by the logon task, still ran. The uninstaller and the upgrade now turn the logon task off, stop the server and kill one that outlives it (#508, #514), and the smoke test upgrades over a running server and exits with its own verdict. The cause itself (#513): a stop was read only by the main loop, while start-up spent about 24 s in Tailscale's probe on a computer without Tailscale (six local addresses one after another, each waiting out 2 s), and a LAN scan blocked a runtime thread; start-up now races the stop, the scan awaits, the runtime's shutdown is bounded at 2 s, and `stop` waits for the process itself on Windows: 27 s to 0.4 s from stop to exit. The probe also kept the control port closed those 24 s after every start, so a phone could not pair then; it runs beside start-up now (#515). `internet_path` (desktop Settings "Reachable from the internet") turns off the relays, the DHT and the router's port mapping (#507), and `docs/privacy.md`, shown by the installer before it installs, says what goes where and how to turn each off (#509): both for SignPath's free code signing, applied for after the launch. The AUR package `pocketdesk` is published (maintainer `xenoz`, #510, #511), the winget manifests carry 0.7.0 (#512), and the F-Droid metadata (full commit hash, LF endings) is merge request fdroid/fdroiddata!54623, its CI waiting on a maintainer. Not here: `TailscaleClient::status()` still says Running when nothing answers and discovery polls it every 10 s; the mDNS browse runs with `lan_discovery = false`.

- 2026-10-27: **BG5, the internet road gets the home road's QUIC tuning on both ends.** The phone's iroh dial set only the congestion controller (BG4). So it kept QUIC's default limit of 100 streams the desktop may open at once, where the home road allows 1024. The video opens one stream per frame, so on a lossy link, with frames waiting on lost packets, the desktop could run out and the video stall behind it. One config, `iroh_transport`, now tunes both iroh endpoints, the phone's dial and the desktop's: 1024 uni and 128 bidi streams, 16 MB datagram buffers, a 45 s idle timeout as on the home road (iroh's keepalive every 5 s holds the paths), and the home road's congestion controller. A new test has a desktop open 300 frame streams to the phone's endpoint, none read yet; it fails on the old config at the 101st and passes now. Tested with the workspace tests and core's with `wan`; the phone's run by the internet road is owed, batched with the next items.
- 2026-10-27: **BG11, the phone draws a picture when it is decoded.** The BG plan's row BG4; the history's BG4 went to BBR. The phone's decoder took pictures out only right after it fed a frame, so a picture still decoding waited for the next frame. On a still desktop that is the once-a-second heartbeat. Now, while a picture is due (`PendingOutputs`), the bridge is polled every 4 ms instead of every 50 ms and the decoder is drained each time, so the picture is drawn a few ms after it comes out. Feeding never waits on the decoder: a first version that waited for the picture after each batch kept the next frames out of the decoder meanwhile. A feed with no picture after 100 ms counts as dropped, so it cannot keep the polling short. When several pictures are ready at once the phone is behind, and only the newest is drawn. On the OPPO, the session record's decode time (feed to picture), same server and same bridge, `main` against this: on an idle desktop (two runs each), p50 72 and 42 ms against 19 and 21, p95 1512 and 168 ms against 46 and 37, max 1706 and 1317 ms against 94 and 109; on the moving test pattern, p50 23 against 22 ms, p95 72 against 59 ms, max 532 against 115 ms. The HUD's delay is measured before the phone's decoder, so it does not show this. Telling the decoder the stream's frame rate, the row's last part, is left: it is a hint whose effect differs by device. Tested with JVM unit tests for `PendingOutputs`, `assembleDebug`, `lintDebug` and the Kotlin unit suite.
- 2026-10-27: **BG10, frames kept on the GPU from capture to NVENC, with `inproc-windows`.** From the BG plan (lighter capture). Every captured frame on Windows went through a staging texture, a map and a row copy to reach the CPU, only for in-process NVENC to send it back to the GPU. Now, when the screen is on an NVIDIA adapter, the capture copies each frame into one of a few textures of its own (`GpuRing`; a texture is reused once no frame holds it) and the frame carries that texture (`VideoFrame::gpu`). On the first such frame, in-process NVENC reopens on a D3D11 frames context of the capture's device, then copies only the frame's region into its input texture, so a window crop is a narrower copy. Keyframes on request and bitrate changes in place work as before. Everything else that needs the pixels reads them back (`VideoFrame::downloaded`): the sidecar, the software rung, and NVENC itself when it cannot open on that device (another vendor's adapter, such as a laptop's integrated GPU). The device's immediate context is made multithread-protected, since the encoder uses it from its own thread. `POCKETDESK_ZERO_COPY=0` turns it off. A spike first: on this box's NVIDIA GPU at 2560x1080, 4.3 ms a frame against 9.4 ms with the readback. On the OPPO against the same release build, `au2-hud-delay` on an idle desktop, two runs each: delay median 6 and 7 ms with frames on the GPU, 23 and 15 ms without; the server's CPU had a mean of 4 % of one core against 6-7 %, a p90 of 10 % against 16-19 %, and 15 busy seconds against 29-32. A first run with it on did not link by the internet road, and its log was lost before it could be read; the two that followed linked and passed. Tested: on the software (WARP) device, a cropped GPU frame reads back exactly its region and the ring reuses only free textures; live, NVENC takes 60 cropped GPU frames with a keyframe first and on request and the bitrate changed in place, and the WGC, DXGI and resized-window captures pass with their frames read back. Off by default with the feature; the Linux captures are unchanged.
- 2026-10-27: **BG9 (second part), frame buffers reused.** Each captured frame on Windows had a new 11 MB buffer (at 2560x1080), its pages faulted in again on the first write. A dropped frame now gives its buffer back to a small pool (`frame_pool`, at most four kept) while a capture runs, and the copier's next frame takes it; when the last capture ends the pool is emptied, so nothing is held between sessions. Filling a frame went from 1.76 ms to 0.83 ms in a bench of the same copy. A window crop, which runs on every frame of a cropped session, packs its rows in place instead of into a second buffer. Tested on a software (WARP) device and in unit tests, and the live capture tests (WGC, DXGI, the resized still window) pass; not yet run on the phone. The Linux captures do not take from the pool yet.
- 2026-10-27: **BG9 (first part), a lighter capture.** Asked for more quality from fewer resources, the server's cost was measured where it is spent. On this box's 2560x1080 output, each captured frame cost, on the CPU: the staging copy and map (4.2 ms), its rows into a new buffer (2.2 ms), and a clone of all 11 MB kept only so the once-a-second idle heartbeat could repeat it (1.7 ms). The heartbeat now reads the last frame again from the copier's staging texture, which already holds it (`StagingCopy::copy_again`), one read a second instead of a copy per frame, on WGC and DXGI alike. Tested on a software (WARP) device; the live capture tests pass, the resized still window too (it leans on the heartbeat); on the OPPO, `au2-hud-delay` passed on an idle desktop (delay median 18 ms), the server at 61 MB. Next: frame buffers reused instead of allocated each frame (1 ms each), then BG10, frames kept on the GPU from capture to NVENC (this box's screen and NVENC share the NVIDIA adapter).
- 2026-10-27: **BG8, NVENC in-process on Windows, behind `inproc-windows`.** From the BG plan (streaming quality), moved into the cycle once the bench showed what the FFmpeg sidecar cost. On Windows every hardware encoder had run as an FFmpeg child fed BGRA on stdin: it could not be asked for a keyframe (a loss waited up to 2 s) and each change of rate restarted it (about 500 ms without frames). With the `inproc-windows` feature (off by default), the in-process encoder runs NVENC too: BGRA straight to `h264_nvenc`/`hevc_nvenc`, zero delay, a keyframe on the frame asked for, and the rate changed in place, which the streamer now uses instead of a rebuild. `ffmpeg-next` comes through `ffmpeg-shim/`, outside the workspace, so a plain build never needs FFmpeg's development files; `docs/development-setup.md` §4a has the recipe (LLVM for libclang, FFmpeg 8 shared, the MinGW bindgen arguments) and says to ship an LGPL FFmpeg. On the lossy-link bench at 2 % loss, 1080p60: 60 fps against the sidecar's 53, no stalls of 200 ms against about 12, e2e p99 32 ms against 235-285, and the encoder keeping up with the screen (67 captures replaced against 400-560). On the OPPO (a throwaway, the GPU otherwise idle): `au2-hud-delay` delay median 18 ms against the sidecar's 34-53 ms, encode median 9 ms against 21; `an1-presets` two changes in place, the one rebuild (a frame rate) 214 ms against 465-615. From the review: the software path is rebuilt as before (libx265 reads its rate only when it opens), and in-process NVENC is named `in-process-nvenc` in the record. Run while other work held the GPU at 97 %, the same checks were slow and failed, so GPU contention matters when reading these numbers.
- 2026-10-27: **BG4, BBR paces the video on both roads.** From the BG plan (streaming quality), found with the lossy-link bench. On a link that loses packets at random, as Wi-Fi does, NewReno (the default) halved its window at each loss and the video queued behind it. At 2 % loss, 20 Mbit/s, the e2e p95 was 350-435 ms with NewReno and 32-47 ms with BBR, about a clean link's; the p99 was 620-740 ms against 235-285 ms. A clean link is unchanged. On a 1 Mbit/s link neither controller wins consistently: runs vary widely, the frame rate swings between 15 and 27 fps with either, and both are slow (p95 0.5-1.1 s), because the bitrate controller, not the congestion controller, is what is slow there; that is the next item. The choice (`congestion_choice`) is BBR unless `POCKETDESK_CC` names another (`new_reno` goes back), and it now reaches the internet road too: the server's iroh endpoint, which sends the video, and the phone's dial endpoint (noq-proto's BBR3). On the OPPO against a throwaway by the internet road, this branch's server and bridge: `au2-hud-delay` (delay median 34 ms) and `an1-presets` passed, the throwaway logging BBR.
- 2026-10-27: **BG, a measuring stick for streaming: a test pattern and a lossy-link bench.** With `POCKETDESK_TEST_PATTERN` set, capture makes a moving pattern (a gradient, a bar that moves each frame, a strip counting frames) instead of reading the desktop, so a bench has a picture that changes every frame and a throwaway's session shows no one's screen. `server/tests/lossy_link_smoothness.rs` (ignored, run by hand) runs the real server with it through the chaos proxy and prints how evenly frames arrive and the session's record. Baseline on main (NVENC sidecar, 1080p60, 20 Mbit/s, 10 ms each way): a clean link held 60 fps, gaps p99 28 ms, e2e p99 32 ms; with 2 % loss, 53 fps, gaps p99 149 ms (max 485), 13 stalls of 200 ms or more in 40 s, e2e p95 435 ms and p99 623 ms, and no freezes, since a frame is never lost, only late. The BG plan gains the baseline and moves an in-process Windows encoder into the cycle (BG8): the FFmpeg sidecar cannot be asked for a keyframe and restarts on each change of rate.
- 2026-10-27: **BG1, a session's record says when its picture froze.** From the BG plan (streaming quality); measured first, so the next items can show what they change. The client times each freeze, from the first lost frame it notices (it asks for a keyframe) to the keyframe that restarts the picture, and reports them as a new sample kind (`SAMPLE_RECOVERY`). The server keeps them as `freeze_*` in the session's record (count and tail; absent when there was none, never a zero), beside `trimmed`, the frames its transport cut back to a newer keyframe, and `replaced`, the captured frames a newer one replaced before the encoder took them (BG2's slot). `pocketdesk sessions` says how many sessions froze. On the OPPO against a throwaway by the internet road (`au2-hud-delay`, 30 s, NVENC sidecar): no freeze, `trimmed=0`, `replaced=34` (the encoder was busier than the screen changed, and now takes the newest frame), HUD delay median 32 ms.
- 2026-10-27: **BG2, the encoder takes the newest captured frame.** From the BG plan (streaming quality). Capture pushed frames into a channel of two and, when it was full, dropped the frame it had just made: the encoder worked through older frames, and the last change on the screen could wait for the next repeat (up to a second on a still screen). A one-frame slot (`core::streaming::latest_frame`) now holds the newest, and a new frame replaces the one waiting, on every backend (WGC, DXGI, the portal, screencopy, X11). It keeps the channel's `try_send`, so the backends' code is unchanged, and counts the frames it replaced, for BG1's record. The runner's session checks on the OPPO against a throwaway by the internet road, BG2's server: `au2-hud-delay` (HUD delay median 30 ms at an 18 ms round trip), `an1-presets` (four presets mid-session, no redial) and `ae2-crops` (12 crops, no redial).
- 2026-10-27: **`pocketdesk ask` says at once when no phone may answer (a BF3 follow-up).** Ask is off for every phone until the owner turns it on, so a first `pocketdesk ask` waited out its whole time (2 minutes) and said only "No answer in time". With no paired device that has the permission, it now exits 2 at once and says to turn on "Answer my questions" on the Devices page; a trust store that does not read is not taken as "none".
- 2026-10-27: **BF4, a note to the computer.** From the BF plan (new features). Text or a link shared to "Note to computer" in the phone's share sheet is added, with the computer's local date and time, to a Markdown file the owner picks in the desktop app's Settings ("Notes from phones go in", `notes_file`; a file picker on Windows): one list item a note, its later lines indented inside it, the file made if its folder is there. With no file picked the phone says to pick one. Each phone needs the new "Add to my notes" permission, off for every device until the owner turns it on (not shown for a paired computer). The phone sends `kdeconnect.pocketdesk.note {text}` and waits for `note_saved`, so it says whether the note was kept, and why not (no file, empty, not written), in en, es and ta; a note's words are never logged. The phone's Settings "Note to computer" takes the entry out of the share sheet. From the review: notes had ridden the Files permission, which someone else's phone and a guest have, so they could have written in the owner's notes (now their own permission); a refused phone waited 10 s for a vague failure (now told at once); each note read the whole notes file (now its last byte); the share entry's switch did not move when tapped; a quoted path pasted from Windows was refused; a note's bidi marks are dropped. The runner's `bf4-note-to-computer` (§7) shares a made-up line through the alias: with the permission off the phone is told so at once, with no file picked it says to pick one, and with the throwaway's own file picked the line is added with the time.
- 2026-10-27: **BF3, `pocketdesk ask`: a Yes or No from the owner's phone.** From the BF plan (new features). `pocketdesk ask [--wait SECS] <question…>` puts a question to each linked phone with the new Ask permission and waits, 2 minutes unless told, at most an hour. It prints `yes` or `no` and exits 0, 1, or 2 for no answer in time, so a script, a deploy or a git hook can wait on the owner's say. Ask is off for every phone until the owner turns on "Answer my questions" on its Devices page (not shown for a paired computer). The question goes through `asks_out/`; the server puts it to every linked phone with the permission, and again on each link a phone makes while it waits; the first answer from a phone it was put to goes to `asks_in/`, and any other is dropped. The phone offers it in a notification, with no session open, only from its own computer, its words hidden on the lock screen (switch "Questions from the computer", on), counting the wait on its own clock. No is one tap; Yes takes the phone's fingerprint or screen lock (BiometricPrompt on Android 11 and later, the lock's own screen before), so a phone without a lock can only say No. The question's words are never logged. From the review: Ask had been on by default, so a guest, someone else's phone or a paired computer paired before it would have gained the right to say Yes (now off until chosen, with a test for an entry from before it); a write into a link already dead had counted the phone as asked, so a phone that linked again was never asked (now each new link is); a session with another computer took the phone's questions and judged them against that computer (both places now judge against the phone's own computer); the phone's clock ahead of the computer's dropped a question as expired; a rotation mid-prompt lost a Yes; a double tap opened two prompts; the CLI's wait is on the monotonic clock; and two Spanish strings said the opposite. The runner's `bf3-ask` (§8) asks through a throwaway, the phone's permission turned on for it: on the OPPO, which has no screen lock, Yes is refused and says why, and No at a tap ends the command with 1 and `no`. Yes behind a real lock is owed by hand.
- 2026-10-27: **BF2, Peek: the computer's screen as one still from the phone's Home.** From the BF plan (new features). "Peek" in each computer's menu on the phone shows one still of its main screen in a sheet, with no session; a tap or Look again takes a new one, never on its own. The phone asks `kdeconnect.pocketdesk.peek` with a transfer key, and the computer takes the still (xcap), shrinks it to 1600 on its longest side and sends it as a JPEG through the transfer path, as a clipboard image is fetched. A new per-phone permission, Peek, is off for every phone until its owner turns it on (Devices; not shown for a paired computer), and desktop Settings "Phones may peek at this screen" (`peek`, on) holds it for all; a pause holds it too. Refusals reach the phone as reasons it words in en, es and ta: off, paused, busy, too soon (one still per phone every 2 s), no screen, not allowed. The phone reports its own "Peek at the computer" switch (L7, M2). From the review: the still has 8 s (a desktop's screenshot portal can ask at the screen and wait forever, which had held the phone's connection and every later peek), a stand-in path that is not absolute refuses rather than taking the screen, the 2 s gap counts only a still taken, each peek on the phone has its own cache file (one from a sheet closed and opened again deleted the other's), a leftover goes when the sheet opens, and a picture larger than a screen is not decoded. The runner's `bf2-peek` (§20) peeks at a throwaway's stand-in still (`POCKETDESK_PEEK_FILE`, always set for throwaways, so a check never takes this computer's screen); a real screen's still is owed by hand.
- 2026-10-27: **BF1, `pocketdesk done` tells the phone when a command ends.** From the BF plan (new features). `pocketdesk done -- <command>` runs a command, its output passing through, and returns the command's own exit code; `--pid N` waits on a process already running. When it ends, the phones that show the computer's notifications get one with how it ended (Done, Failed and its code, Stopped), how long it took and its last three lines. The phone shows these alerts with no session open, from its background link (the switch "Alerts from the computer", on), and keeps that link for them. From the review: a program the command left running in the background no longer holds `done` (the output gets a two-second grace), `.cmd` and `.bat` scripts such as `npm` are found as a shell finds them, `--title` names the alert instead of the command line (which can carry a secret, and may show on the lock screen), and both output streams keep their own half-lines. The device check found two older faults behind the computer's notifications, both fixed here: the server's connection marked a relayed notification as from the phone it went to, so the phone dropped it as its own and the computer's notifications reached a phone only in a session; and on Android 14 a notification whose Reply carries an immutable intent is refused, so none was ever posted on the OPPO (its reply intent is now mutable, and an alert has no Reply). The runner's `bf1-done-tells-the-phone` (§8) runs a command through the throwaway's `pocketdesk done` and finds its notification on the OPPO.
- 2026-10-27: **BE, a sweep of the runner on main with no sound.** After the BB to BD cycles changed the phone app in many places, the runner's short checks ran on the OPPO against a throwaway, built from main (the APK and bridge from `e460c218`'s tree, the server from `6b0d1b85`). The eight checks that play or touch sound (the siren, ring, ringer, alarm, mic and media checks) were left out at the owner's word, and the long ones were held for a batch with the next features. Of 62: 52 passed, 8 skipped (switches not on), and 2 timed out waiting for the phone to link by the internet road (`w1-away-connect`, `w1-caption`; two other checks that ride it passed in the same run). A first pass also missed `am1-sticky-software` (this computer ran out of memory: "the paging file is too small"), `at1-someone-else`, `w1-reset` and `w2-quiet`, all passing when run again with nothing else building. Nothing to fix was found; the internet road's slow link stays to be looked at with the long batch.
- 2026-10-26: **The BD cycle ends (plan removed, in git history).** An audit of what a computer keeps for another of the owner's computers once it leaves or changes (the computer-side mirror of AX to BC) found six things, each fixed and unit-tested: a computer paired here the other way kept its Computers entry, which took every send to it by a road that was down (BD1, #473); files offered when the server stopped stayed "waiting" for good (BD2, #472); the last pairing's line outlived Unpair, and a half-written request (a Pair one holds the PIN) was never removed (BD3, #475); rows for an away computer showed its id (BD4, #474); with the internet road off every computer read "connecting…" for good (BD5, #476); and a computer paired again at new addresses kept its old pins (BD6, #478). BD7 (#477) made a share test that flaked on Windows CI close its phone side in order. With the OPPO back on USB, BC3's check ran on it and passed. Not in it: clipboard history keeping a forgotten computer's id as an entry's source, and `pocketdesk unpair` leaving `computers.json` (left in BA).
- 2026-10-26: **BD6, a computer paired again at new addresses drops its old pins.** From the BD audit. Pairing again with a computer this one had paired with replaced its entry, but the pins it had set for its old addresses stayed in `known_peers.json` for good, and `pocketdesk connect-peer` to one of them read as a changed certificate. Once the new entry is recorded, the pins the old one set go, but those at its new addresses and any another computer holds now (`drop_pins`, shared with Unpair, unit-tested).
- 2026-10-26: **BD7, the broken-share test closes its phone side in order.** Found on BD5's CI: `a_broken_share_is_continued_by_the_phones_retry` failed once on Windows, the desktop having kept fewer than the 120 000 bytes the test phone sent before breaking off. The test phone dropped its socket with the desktop's answers unread, and Windows then resets the connection, losing bytes still in flight. On a cut-short send it now shuts its side and reads to the end before closing (test only); the share tests passed 25 runs of 25 on Windows. BD5 had been merged with that job failing, as its merge followed the watch unconditionally; merges now check every job green on the PR's head first.
- 2026-10-26: **BD5, with the internet road off, a computer's link says so.** From the BD audit. Links to the owner's other computers ride the internet road; when it did not start on this computer, no link was ever tried, and every computer read "connecting…" for good. With the road off, each says "not linked (the internet road is off on this computer)" from the start, and so does one paired meanwhile (unit-tested). From the review: the road is set once per run, so the words stay true until a restart.
- 2026-10-26: **BD3, the last pairing's line goes with its computer, and a half-written request goes once stale.** From the BD audit. The Computers page's "Last pairing, with Desk" stayed after Desk was unpaired, for good, with any address a failure named; and a request the app left half-written in `computers_out/` (a crash mid-write; a Pair one holds the PIN) was never removed. The last pairing now records the computer its link named, and Unpair clears it with that computer (`ComputersFile::remove`, unit-tested; a line from before is matched by name); a half-written request older than a request's two-minute life is removed as requests are read (unit-tested), so one being written now stays. From the review: a request whose rename fails removes its half-written file at once.
- 2026-10-26: **BD4, a row for a paired computer that is away is named by its name.** From the BD audit. The Files page named a computer only while its link was up, so a sent row for one that was only away read as its id's first characters. Every computer this one paired with now names its rows ("Desk (computer)"), linked or not, server running or not; only a linked one is a place to send (unit-tested). From the review: an away computer is never offered as a target.
- 2026-10-26: **BD1, a computer that pairs here is no longer one this computer paired with.** From the BD audit. Computers pair one way (2f), and pairing with a link from a computer already trusted here is refused, but the other way was not checked: a computer this one had paired with, unpaired there and then paired back this way, kept its Computers entry, so every send to it went by the computer road and ended "not linked now" though it was connected here, and it was redialed every five minutes. A computer that pairs here is now asked forgotten as the app's Unpair does, its link and pins with it (`peer_computers::paired_here`, unit-tested). From the review: until that is taken, a send to it goes by the device road when it is connected here and its computer link is down; and a pairing from here that finds the computer paired here meanwhile (both at once) fails and puts its pins back, so only one way is kept.
- 2026-10-26: **BD2, a file offered when the server stopped is marked not sent at the next start.** From the BD audit. The sent list keeps each file the computer offers a phone or another computer as "waiting to be taken" until it is, but the offers live in memory: one made before the server stopped could never be taken, and its row said "waiting" for good, with no Send again (offered only for a failed send). At start, before the outbox is read, every row still offered is failed, "the server stopped before it was taken" (`sent::settle_offered`, unit-tested), so Send again offers it once its device is back. From the review: nothing reloads an offer after a restart (the phone's offers again and the computer road both replay memory only), the outbox's own sends are recorded after the settle, and a row already failed, with its note, is kept as it was.
- 2026-10-26: **The BC cycle ends (plan removed, in git history).** An audit of every other way a saved computer leaves or changes on the phone found six things, each fixed and unit-tested: the doctor's Pair again did less than Remove (BC1, #465); a removed computer stayed other cards' wake relay, and could have been asked to send their wakes once another took its address (BC2, #466); a found computer that never paired, or a pairing link that failed, left a certificate pin with no card to remove it (BC3, #467); kept texts went to whichever computer came next (BC4, #468); a computer's word that the phone was not paired outlived its key (BC5, #469); and the bridge kept an unread list of every computer ever paired (BC6, #470). The OPPO dropped off USB during the cycle: BC1's and BC3's device checks are owed on the checklist, BC3's runner check written but not yet run. Not in it: a widget's kept reading shown for a minute for a computer saved at the last one's old address.
- 2026-10-26: **BC6, the bridge keeps no unread list of paired desktops.** From the BC audit. Each pairing added the computer's device id to `paired_servers.json` in the phone's certificate folder; nothing read it (its only reader, `RustCore.pairedServers`, had no caller) and nothing took a computer out when it was removed, so it listed every computer the phone had ever paired with. Its writer, reader, JNI export and Kotlin wrapper are gone, and the file is removed as the bridge's data folder is set at start (unit-tested); `docs/protocol.md`'s list of the phone's files no longer names it.
- 2026-10-26: **BC5, a computer's word that the phone is not paired goes with its key, and with a yes to pairing.** From the BC audit. The bridge keeps a computer's word that the phone is not paired with it (BB1), and holds every send to it but pairing's (BB2), until that computer sends something else or the phone pairs there by PIN. Two ways missed it: a computer removed on the phone left its word, so a different computer saved later at that address was held until it sent something other than pairing; and a pairing finished from the computer's side never cleared it. Forgetting a computer's certificate (Remove, Pair again, a move) now forgets its word too, and pairing's answer that they are paired (`kdeconnect.pair` with `pair: true`, sent only once the computer trusts the phone) lets the phone in (unit-tested). From the review: the forget test keeps to its own certificate folder, under the lock its sibling holds.
- 2026-10-26: **BC4, a kept text goes to the computer it was kept for.** From the BC audit. With "Show texts" on, incoming texts (sender and body) are kept, up to 200, until the phone's own computer takes them, and they went to whichever computer was the own one when they left: texts kept while the own computer was away went to the next one once it was removed, and turning "Show texts" off kept them, so they were sent when it was turned on again. Each text now names the own computer it arrived for (its paired id) and goes to that computer alone; one kept for a computer since removed is dropped, and turning the switch off drops them all (`TextQueue`, unit-tested). From the review: a text kept for a computer that then said the phone is not paired goes to the own one, as the phone's other sends do since BB2 (a fresh process, not yet told, tagged it for that computer and it waited for good); the flush sends only while the own computer is still the one it chose; and the delivered texts leave the queue by what they are, not as its first ones, so one kept meanwhile stays.
- 2026-10-26: **BC3, a computer that never paired leaves no pin behind.** From the BC audit. A session to a computer found on the network pins its key on first use, and the computer is saved only once it pairs; a pairing link with no fingerprint pins the same way, and its undo took back only a pin from the code. One that never paired kept its pin with no card to remove it, so another computer at that address (or the same one reinstalled) got "this phone already trusts a different computer at X; remove it first", with nothing to remove short of clearing the app's data. A failed pairing link with no fingerprint now forgets the pin its connection made, unless a saved computer's pin is there (`PairOps.hasCard`, unit-tested), and a session that ends with no card saved at its address forgets that address's pin (`SessionPins`, unit-tested). From the review: the forget waits three seconds, so the session's own teardown cannot pin it again, and keeps the pin of a session opened again to it meanwhile; a sweep of every pin at start was weighed and left out, as pins are kept by TLS name and could not be matched to cards safely, so a found session ended by swiping the app away still keeps its pin. The runner's `bc3-unsaved-pin-forgotten` (§1) is written, but has not run: the phone was off USB, so its check is owed.

AX2 fixed this for a move only. Remove now clears that relay on every card (`WakePlan.relayRemoved`, unit-tested), in the loop the move uses, so their plan is "no relay" and Wake is hidden until another is chosen. - 2026-10-26: **BB4, a camera photo or screenshot that failed goes again.** From the BB audit. "Send new camera photos" and the Downloads half of "New screenshots to the computer" handed each file to the transfer service and took it for sent: one whose transfer failed (the computer went away mid-file, or unpaired the phone) was never sent again. The service now keeps how each file an uploader handed over went (`SendOutcomes`: sent, failed or cancelled), and the uploader settles its batch once the service is through: a file that failed, or that the service never ran, goes again after a rest, up to three sends in all, then is given up and logged; one cancelled on either side, or refused by the computer, does not (`UploadRetry`, unit-tested). Marks still move as files are handed over, so one that arrived never goes twice. From two reviews: a first design that moved the marks only past what arrived sent every later photo again behind one that kept failing, undid a cancel and pushed a screenshot to the computer's clipboard on each retry, so the marks stayed and failures go on a list instead; only files an uploader hands over are kept, registered before the service starts, with a minute's grace for a service not started yet; a retry keeps its rest; a screenshot goes again to Downloads only; and what waited goes with the switch turned off.
- 2026-10-26: **BB5, a computer that keeps refusing is dialed less often.** From the BB audit. Away from home, a computer that was off, or that unpaired the phone, was tried by the internet road once a minute for good: an endpoint, relays and perhaps the DHT each time, with the link's service keeping the app awake for it. Each failure in a row now doubles the rest, from a minute to sixteen; a link that comes up, a new network and the person's Connect on Home start it again (`internet_rest`, `InternetPause`, unit-tested with the window a run is kept for and the cap). From the review: a network back after a loss is a change too (the loss alone had cleared the rests while nothing could be reached), and Home's Connect lets that computer's rest go, so one that woke is reached at once.

A connection whose device was let in on it is now closed at its next refused packet, or within a minute when idle, once the device is no longer let in (`closes`, unit-tested); one never let in stays open, so an unpaired phone can still pair on it, and with pairing off nothing closes. - 2026-10-26: **AZ2, the deck widget keeps a paired computer's list by its paired id.** From AZ's plan. The widget kept its list, and drew its buttons, for an address. A computer saved at the address of the one whose list it showed inherited that list: its buttons, pressed, went to the new computer, which ran its own action when it had the same label at that index. A removal with no computer left kept the list on disk. The list is now kept for a paired computer's paired id, or an unpaired one's address (`DeckWidgetPlan.computerKey`, `forgets`, unit-tested). - 2026-10-26: **AY4, a guest grant's end fires `phone_unpaired`.** From the unpair audit. The watcher read the trust store again only when the file changed, and a time-boxed guest grant ends without a write, so its `phone_unpaired` event, and any hook on it, waited for some other change to the store. It now reads the store again once the soonest grant then read has ended too (`TrustStore::next_grant_end`, `paired_list_due`, both unit-tested). The runner's `ay4-grant-end` (§79) writes a 20-second grant for an id no phone has into a throwaway's trust store: AY4's server logged `phone_paired` at once and `phone_unpaired` as the grant ended, 0 s late; main's logged no `phone_unpaired`. The store is put back byte for byte. From the review: the check waits for the watcher's first look, which is silent, before writing, and fails an unpair logged before the grant ended. Also `docs/local-api.md` names `task_refused`'s reasons since AY2.
- 2026-10-26: **AY3, ending a phone's sessions ends its own alone.** From the unpair audit. The desktop app ended a phone's sessions, on Unpair and when a permission it started with was turned off, by its id as a kick target. As a target, "all" or an id's prefix of 6 characters or more names other devices' sessions too, so a phone whose id is "all", or a prefix of another's, would have ended every session or that other one. A device's own kick is a request of its own now (`request_kick_device`, `kicks/*.device`), and the server ends that device's sessions alone (`sessions::kick_device`); both unit-tested. `pocketdesk kick` keeps its targets. The runner's `ay3-device-kick` (§2a) makes the app's request in a session on the OPPO: one for a device named "all" and one for a prefix of the phone's id left the session up, and its own id ended it in 0.5 s. Main's server, which does not know the request, kept it. From the review: the Sessions page's End takes the same request for a session with a device id (by its address otherwise); an older server still ends an unpaired or narrowed phone's session by its session id.
- 2026-10-26: **AY2, a phone's task runs and reports only while the phone may run tasks.** From the unpair audit. A task the owner allowed after its phone was unpaired, or its tasks turned off, still ran. Its state and up to 64 KiB of its output went to the phone's connection, which an unpair blocks but does not close; so did a task already running when the phone was unpaired. A phone's task, its own start or its deck's, now runs only while the phone is let in (paired, or pairing not required) and allowed tasks, checked after the owner's answer. A phone let in whose tasks were turned off is told "not permitted"; one no longer let in is told nothing, and none of its reports goes (`Reply::standing`, unit-tested). Home Assistant's task is not a phone's and is unaffected. From AY1's review: a report goes only to a connection the push filter lets through, never to a newer one claiming the id with another key. From the review: a phone unpaired while pairing is off still runs tasks, as before, and one whose tasks are off is told why. On the OPPO, against a server with it, the runner's task checks still passed (`o1-task`, `o1-deck`).
- 2026-10-26: **AY1, the v2 control stream judges each packet by its key too.** From an audit of what an unpaired phone keeps on the computer. The v2 control stream checked a phone's key only at its handshake, where an unpaired id has no key to check, and then asked each packet only whether the id is paired. A session opened claiming an unpaired phone's id, with another key (someone on the network who knew the id), would have acted with that phone's permissions once the real phone paired again and bound its own key. The TCP gate already re-read both for each packet. Now one rule (`pair::may_act`, `Standing::may_act` unit-tested) judges each packet for the TCP gate, the push filter and the v2 stream, a connection that proved an iroh key held to the internet road's rule. From the review: a v2 session without a client certificate is refused, as v2 never binds a key and a paired id with none bound would act by its claim alone; Pocketdesk's own clients always present one, and the app does not use v2 at all. Against a server with it, the runner's session and pairing checks passed on the OPPO: a session by the road, the HUD, the siren, an unpaired phone by the road, and an unpaired phone's reports.

- 2026-10-26: **AU2, the HUD's delay on an idle desktop.** The runner's `au2-hud-delay` (§2b) opens a session on the OPPO by the internet road, with the phone's input off, and once the round trip is measured, reads the HUD's delay and rtt for 20 s from the screen. It requires the delay's median under 100 ms, about half the round trip or more in most samples (the delay is the frame's age plus half of it), and the delay not a copy of the round trip, the old bug's shape. Four runs: delay 16 to 19 ms against an rtt of 6 to 7 ms. On an idle desktop the frames are the capture's repeats, so the delay here is their encode and queue, not the compositor's age. From the review: the round trip counts as measured by the caption (one under 1 ms reads 0), a delay of 0 before the first frame is no sample, and the delay's average may lag a few ms. §2b's rows stay open for the LAN and for heavy motion: no LAN session runs through adb, and motion would need input on this computer.

- 2026-10-25: **AR3, AP1 puts Do Not Disturb back.** Found by AR2's sweep. Nine checks that send a notification from the phone timed out (m6, n3, n4, p3, p4, p5, o5), as the phone's Do Not Disturb was on, enabled by the fixture app. AP1 sets the ringer to silent through the fixture, which turns Do Not Disturb on, and it put back only the ringer. The phone then held back every notification the later checks sent, as its "hold back while Do Not Disturb" rule says. AP1 now reads Do Not Disturb before it starts (`zen_mode`) and sets it back after with `cmd notification set_dnd`. On the OPPO, Do Not Disturb read 0 before and after, and the nine checks passed once it was off. On this phone silent is Do Not Disturb: silent with it off cannot be set, and turning it off moves the ringer to vibrate. Where both cannot be put back, Do Not Disturb wins, as the later checks need notifications, and the check notes the ringer (from the review). The OPPO, silent before AP1, is on vibrate now.
- 2026-10-25: **AR1, a replaced control link's state is not read as the new computer's.** The audit after AQ3 found the rest of its kind. When the phone's control link moved to another computer, the old link's reader skipped its cleanup, as it was no longer the live one, and the new connection forgot nothing. The first computer's pairing answer, file offers, reply requests, links, commands, dials and the rest were then read as the second's. A late approval from one computer could be saved as another's pairing. Now the connection that replaces one to another computer forgets what the old one carried: one `forget_link`, shared with the reader's own end. A replaced reader handles no line after it is replaced (`is_live`, unit-tested). The same computer dialed again keeps what is still its own. From the review: a dying reader asked whether it was live before taking the state's lock, which a dial holds while it connects, so a dial that succeeded meanwhile could be wiped. It now asks under the state's and the writer's locks. On the OPPO with this build: the whole runner, 42 PASS, 0 FAIL, the SKIPs AO1's (AR2). Nine notification checks timed out at first because AP1 had left Do Not Disturb on (AR3), and passed once it was off.

A second ring, left alone, stopped both 34 and 35 s after it was asked, in two runs: the ring's 30 s from when the phone took it. That ring comes a minute after the first, as the computer rings a phone at most once a minute; a first try that rang again at once found no dialog for that reason. The alarm is read from Android's players on the alarm stream. Android plays the ringtone from SystemUI's process, not the app's, so the check requires that none played before each ring. The ring raises the alarm's volume, and the check reads it back where it was (6) after. From the review: the volume is read, not assumed; the minute is counted from the first ring's ask, with a margin; the ring's end is timed from its ask; and a check that stops part way silences a ring still going, or waits it out.

- 2026-10-25: **AC7, a phone's offers wait while it moves another file.** Found by AC3: the phone moves one file at a time, and the server's offer of a file lasted 10 minutes from when it was made, so a send queued behind a 7.5 GiB share by the internet road (10.6 minutes) was refused when the phone came to it, "the phone did not take it in time". Now, while a phone moves a file with the computer either way, each of its open offers has 10 minutes from then (`offers::keep_device_alive`: at a share's or fetch's start, at most once a minute while bytes go, and at a fetch's end; a lapsed offer is not brought back). Unit-tested, and on the OPPO the same 7.5 GiB case then passed. From the review: the fetch's start and end were added, as a queue of short fetches never reached the once-a-minute touch.

The runner now makes the main buffer 16 MiB for a run (`adb logcat -b main -G`) and puts its size back after; a buffer already that big is left alone (review).

- 2026-10-24: **U1's fix: a phone that vanished with data waiting is let go within a minute (branch `fix/vanished-peer`).** Found on the Azure VM (U1, T8's check): the phone left Wi-Fi while the computer had something to tell it, and stayed "linked" for over a quarter of an hour, so "Lock when the phone leaves" waited that long. The control connection's TCP keepalive probes a silent peer within about a minute, but only while nothing waits to be sent; with data unacknowledged, Linux retransmits instead, for 15 minutes or more (seen: 8 KiB queued, 11 retransmissions). Now Linux and Android also set `TCP_USER_TIMEOUT` to 60 s on each control connection, on both ends (`core/src/protocol/connection.rs` `keep_alive`); Windows gives up after its own few retransmissions. Unit-tested where the system says it (Linux CI). Owed: the same Wi-Fi-off check on the VM with this build (checklist §213).

The ask is now said only while access is missing. Checked on the OPPO: with access given, the row has no line under it.
- 2026-10-24: **The T cycle ends (T1 to T8 built; plan removed, in git history).** From what people asked of tools like Pocketdesk in 2025–26, eight gaps closed, each its own PR: starting beside KDE Connect on the next free port (T1, #294), the clipboard on X11 (T2, #295), new camera photos to the computer (T3, #296), the whole photo gallery with pages, videos, several saved and HEIC copied by the phone (T4, #297), folders between computers both ways (T5, #298), files to a KDE Connect device (T6, #299), the phone's gamepad as a game controller on Linux (T7, #300), and the away lock on Sway and Hyprland (T8, #301). Each had a fresh review, every Critical and Important finding fixed before merging (two Critical, both in T7: X and Y swapped for games, and a stick left held). Checked on the OPPO: T1, T3 and T4; T5 with two real servers over the internet road; T2, T7 and T8 type-checked for Linux here, built by CI, owed on a Linux desktop; T6 tested with real TLS on loopback, owed with KDE Connect. **T9 (a second screen and one window on GNOME and KDE through the portal's virtual and window sources) was not built**: mapped and deferred to a session with a GNOME or KDE desktop to build against. The second screen could ride the existing `virtual_screen` answer with a sentinel monitor index the server maps to `SourceType::Virtual` (no phone change), but the input's space on that screen, its size (the portal takes none) and Mutter's and KWin's support are unverified, and the window needs a new phone choice ("choose on the computer") and a session that restarts with `SourceType::Window`; each source kind needs its own restore token. None of it could run here, and it touches the capture path every Linux session takes. Still owner-gated: the phone as a webcam, one-time codes, MMS and group texts, a browser link for devices without the app, receiving from Quick Share, unlocking with the phone's fingerprint.
- 2026-10-24: **T8, lock when the phone leaves on Sway and Hyprland (branch `feat/idle-notify`).** The away lock never locked where the desktop does not say how long the keyboard has been idle (R3: GNOME, KDE's screen saver and X11's `xprintidle` only). Now the compositor says it, where it offers `ext-idle-notify-v1` (Sway, Hyprland, Plasma 6 and other wlroots desktops; `core/src/input/idle_notify.rs`): a Wayland client on a thread of its own asks to be told when no input came for 5 s and when it comes again, and the idle time is counted from that; asked after GNOME's and KDE's answers and before X11's. With version 2 of the protocol it counts input only, so a video playing (an idle inhibitor) does not keep the owner "present". The watch starts as soon as a phone has the switch on (the compositor counts from when it is asked), its connection is waited for 2 s at most, and one that ended or could not start is tried again a minute later; until the compositor first says idle, the owner counts as at the keyboard, so a lock comes later, never early. Fresh review (0 Critical, 2 Important), fixed: the lock came about twice the set time after leaving (the watch began only once a lock could follow), and idle inhibitors kept it from ever locking; plus a stalled compositor holding the away watcher, and a watch that ended restarted at every tick. The Linux code was type-checked and linted for `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu` here against the locked wayland-protocols; CI builds it. Owed: Sway and Hyprland with a phone leaving (checklist §213).
- 2026-10-24: **T7, the phone's gamepad as a game controller on Linux (branch `feat/linux-gamepad`).** The phone's gamepad reached the computer only as keys and the mouse. On Linux, with the new desktop Settings "The phone's gamepad as a game controller" (on by default; `gamepad_controller`, read live), its packets now drive a virtual pad made through uinput at the first one, laid out as `xpad` reports an Xbox 360 controller (its ids, both sticks, the triggers 0–255, the D-pad as a hat, A, B, X, Y as `BTN_SOUTH`/`EAST`/`X`/`Y`, the bumpers, Select, Start, Home and the stick clicks), which games, SDL and Steam map without asking; only what changed is sent, the first packets whole while a game opens it. Without uinput rights, or with the setting off, the gamepad types keys as before, the controller tried again 30 s later. Fresh review (2 Critical, 3 Important), fixed: X and Y the wrong way round for SDL's Xbox mapping, and a stick held in a game when a session ended (the controller outlives sessions: it is now brought to rest, sticks and buttons, as each session ends); plus keys held from before the controller took over, a failed try never retried, and a first press lost while the device appears. Found: the phone sends no L2/R2 yet (the triggers stay at rest). The Linux code was type-checked and linted for `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu` here in a scratch crate; the mapping is unit-tested; CI builds it. Owed: a game or `evtest` on Linux (checklist §212).
- 2026-10-24: **T6, files to a KDE Connect device (branch `feat/files-to-kde`).** The bridge took a KDE device's files but could send it none. Now a paired KDE Connect device is a send target ("Name (KDE Connect)" on the Files page, and `pocketdesk send` by its name or id), and the server sends as KDE Connect does: it listens on one of KDE Connect's payload ports (1739–1764), tells the device on its link with a `kdeconnect.share.request` (`filename`, `lastModified`, `numberOfFiles`, `totalPayloadSize`, `payloadSize`, `payloadTransferInfo.port`), and the device comes for the file from its link's address within a minute, TLS with this computer as the server presenting its KDE certificate and requiring the one the device paired with; the bytes, in pieces each given 30 s. One file at a time to a device (it fetches one at a time), two devices at once; not a folder (KDE Connect has none to take). `pocketdesk install` on Windows opens TCP 1739–1764 for the program. Fresh review (0 Critical, 3 Important), fixed: a second file to a device timed out while the first went, a device that stopped reading held the send for as long as the system kept the connection, and a drop went to a KDE device that was not connected (a drop goes only to a phone or a computer now; a KDE device is chosen); plus the address read after the wait, an IPv6 link said plainly, and KDE Connect's own count and size fields. Tested with real TLS on loopback, each side with its own KDE identity (the file byte for byte; a device with another certificate refused). No KDE Connect device was reachable here (the OPPO has none). Owed: KDE Connect Android and desktop receiving a file, the Windows firewall rule (checklist §211).
- 2026-10-24: **T5, folders between computers both ways (branch `feat/folders-between-computers`).** A folder went only from the computer whose pairing link was used (B) to the other (A): A is B's client on the link, so B offers a folder as it offers one to a phone, but A's sending side (`deliver_to_computer`) refused folders and shared one file at a time with no folder fields. Now A sends a folder as a phone does (T6b): each file a `kdeconnect.share.request` on the link carrying the send's id, the folder's name and the file's path under it, which B already places under one new folder in its Downloads; the files are those a folder offered to a phone would be, less empty ones (B refuses an empty file; said in the row's note), one row in the sent list for the folder, one file after another, the link waited for a few seconds between them, and the first that cannot be sent ends the send with how far it got. A file going to another computer now shows under way in the desktop app, whose Cancel stops it (and the rest of its folder); only the receiving computer could before. Fresh review (0 Critical, 2 Important), fixed: an empty file ended the whole send, and no Cancel on the sending computer; plus a link drop between files failing the rest at once, and stale docs. Checked with two real servers on this computer over the internet road (`two_computers`, ignored by default): a folder with a subfolder and an empty file went from A to B into one new folder, byte for byte, the empty file said, besides the folder and files B to A as before. Owed: two real computers (checklist, second computer).

The camera's videos are listed with the photos with the phone's new "Its videos too" (video access), marked "Video ·", opened in the computer's player and never copied. Photos ticked are saved together ("Save the N chosen", at most 20 at once: the phone keeps 20 requests waiting). A HEIC photo, which the computer cannot decode, is copied by the phone: `fetch {id, clipboard: true}` makes it decode the photo and put a PNG on the computer's clipboard, as new screenshots go. The phone sends only a photo some page it answered listed, since the newest was listed. Fresh review (0 Critical, 1 Important), fixed: more than 20 chosen lost the oldest fetches; plus a page of none listed asking for itself again (`next`), a stale page shown as the phone not answering, and an old phone's share for a Copy treated as unasked. - 2026-10-24: **T2, the clipboard on X11 (branch `feat/x11-clipboard`).** Text and images crossed only through `wl-copy`/`wl-paste`, so an X11 session (Mint and others still start in one) had no clipboard. Now the session's tool is chosen in one place (`core/src/local/clip_tool.rs`): X11 when there is an X display and no Wayland one, Wayland otherwise (a server started with neither, from a unit or over ssh, keeps `wl-clipboard`, which finds `wayland-0` itself). On X11 the reads list `TARGETS` and take the best text target (UTF-8 first) or `image/png` through `xclip`, writes go through `xclip -i` (text as `UTF8_STRING`), and the background watcher hears the clipboard change owner through XFixes on a thread of its own instead of `wl-paste --watch`; copied files and notification replies use the same choice. A password manager's private copy stays here as on Wayland (its hint is among the targets). Fresh review (0 Critical, 2 Important), fixed: a session without `WAYLAND_DISPLAY` taken for X11 (sync dead where it had worked), and reads without a time limit (a hung copy's program stopped every push; now 3 s and killed); plus `xclip`'s text type, notification replies on an X11 box with `wl-clipboard` installed, the watcher's X connection made off the async loop (2 s at most), and a failed copy reported. - 2026-10-24: **T1, start beside KDE Connect (branch `feat/beside-kdeconnect`).** `kdeconnectd` (on every Plasma install) or GSConnect holding TCP 1716 used to stop the server at its bind. Now it takes the next free port up to 1725 (only a port in use moves it on, or on Windows one kept for Hyper-V/WinNAT), says so in its log, writes `<state>/listening.json` (pid, bound and configured port), announces the bound port over mDNS, and leaves its own KDE Connect bridge off while another program holds the port. The desktop app believes the file only for the running server's pid and the configured port it was written for: the pairing code (drawn again with the same PIN when the port changes), the Overview's label, Diagnostics (a "Control port moved" warning with why, and the firewall and report on the bound port). `pocketdesk install` on Windows opens TCP 1716–1725. The phone: a saved computer announced at its own address on another control port, with the id it was paired with, moves its card there once it proves its pinned key on the new port and the old one no longer answers as it; then its other cards for the same id on the old port (a tailnet or Ethernet address mDNS does not announce), each proved at its own address. Fresh review (0 Critical, 3 Important), fixed: a second server for the same folder, once stopped by the bind, now stopped before it takes the pid file; the stream port no longer taken from the unauthenticated record; the computer's other cards followed; plus a pairing code drawn while the server was starting, the report's port, "closed" retried as unreached, Windows' access-denied ports, an install from before (the hint says to run `pocketdesk install` again), and the test's arithmetic. Checked on the OPPO against a throwaway server with its control port held (11/11): it moved to the next port, said why and recorded it, a second start stopped, the phone connected there, and with the port free it went back. Owed: the phone following a moved port it found over mDNS on a LAN, and KDE Connect itself on 1716 on Linux (checklist §207).

  - **The portal:** the tray program binds both in one session with Settings' keys as their preferred triggers (`hotkey::portal_trigger`, tested), and the desktop asks its owner once. A press comes as the portal's `Activated` signal and wakes the tray's loop as `WM_HOTKEY` does on Windows. A change in Settings binds them again in a new session.
  - **Without it:** a desktop with no portal takes no key, and Settings says so ("no global shortcuts portal").
  - **The code:** blocking zbus on threads of its own (`desktop/src/hotkey_portal.rs`), since this crate's zbus runs on async-io. The portal module is type-checked and clippy-clean for `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu`.

  Fresh review (0 Critical, 3 Important, 4 Minor), fixed: an unanswered question to the owner held every later bind (now two minutes, then closed); the program now names itself to the portal with a hidden desktop file of its id; "Registered" only for the keys the desktop took; plus a stale "Registered" while a bind is pending, the presses' thread dying unsaid, and the words saying the desktop may give another key. Windows injects it as its own pen (`CreateSyntheticPointerDevice(PT_PEN)`), Linux as a uinput tablet, elsewhere as the mouse; a pen in contact is lifted when control moves. Checked here: the synthetic pen into a scratch window (enter, down, pressure 313 to 938 of 1024, up, leave). Fresh review (1 Critical, 2 Important), fixed one commit each: prompts and casts unseen with the window closed, a Quit closing the next window, and a Linux claim that could hang a launch. Rows go to `transfers.json` every second while any runs. The Files page shows them under "Under way" with a bar, "42 % · 3.1 MB/s" and Cancel, which writes a request (`transfer_cancels/`).
  - **A Cancel on the computer** stops the transfer as a retry's supersede does and remembers the file once, so the phone's retry is refused ("cancelled on the computer") and stops. A whole folder of offers goes with it.

- 2026-10-17: **N7, the phone's background cost (branch `perf/background-cost`, with as-built and review sections).**
  - **Measured:** an hour each on the A71 against a throwaway server, with the clipboard, files and ringing on and the screen off.
  - **Profiled:** simpleperf put 94 % of the app's cycles in its six timer threads. Each worked out the phone's own computer on every tick, every 2 to 5 s, by reading and parsing the saved computers.
  - **Cut:**
    - that answer is kept 5 s (`Recent`), forgotten at once when the saved computers change (`HostStore.generation`);
    - the folders timer does nothing with no folder shared and nothing asked.
  - **Results:** 21.0 s → 15.9 s of CPU in the hour (0.58 → 0.44 % of one core), no wake locks or alarms either way, deep idle all hour.
  - **These are upper bounds:** USB kept the phone from suspending.
  - **Fresh review (0 Critical, 1 Important), fixed:** a removed computer stayed the phone's own for up to 5 s.
  - **Owed (checklist §102):** on battery without USB, the Wi-Fi keepalives, and a release build.

A release build: 400 sessions, no error, no crash (a 0.75 % bound per session, and no control run without the pin). Fresh review (0 Critical, 5 Important), fixed: the capture fallback never worked (each backend had the session's token, which a failed WGC cancelled; now a child token each), the process now holds COM's MTA for its life (the unloaded-library crash class), and the test's silent passes.

A clean-room bridge from the protocol's behaviour: this computer's own KDE identity (`<state>/certs/kdeconnect/`), broadcast on UDP 1716 when the app's "Find KDE Connect devices" looks; a connection opening with `{` on the control port runs the v8 handshake with the roles reversed (this computer the TLS client, 1.2 or 1.3 on this path only, the device's certificate naming its id, the CVE-2025-66270 check). 3b: pairing both ways held for the owner with the code both screens show (Allow/Deny in Settings), the certificate pinned, ping both ways. **Device-checked with KDE Connect 1.36 on the A71** (a throwaway server): found and listed, codes matching KDE's both ways, paired from either side, pings both ways, unpaired from the phone, reconnecting paired. Found there: a broadcast every 2 s churned the link, a null packet body made KDE drop it, and a device's new link was dropped from the live list by its old one. Fresh 3a review (0 Critical, 5 Important), fixed: the packets test, a race that could corrupt the identity for good, unpaired links unbounded per address, discovery ignoring `lan_discovery`, and a "minute" of looking that was one broadcast. Fresh 3b review (0 Critical, 5 Important), fixed: an Allow taken for a request made since (answers now name the code), a link with another certificate closing a live one, pairing from here finished at the device's yes (now the owner confirms the code), request floods, paired devices kept out by the unpaired places, and a device list that could lose a paired device. **3c:** per-device switches (Clipboard, Battery, Ring on; Files off), the battery kept as a phone's, the clipboard both ways (not `.connect`), Ring, and files the device sends fetched from its address over TLS held to its pin into Downloads. Device-checked on the A71: battery, the clipboard both ways, a file refused with Files off and kept with it on (MD5 matching, marked as downloaded); found there: the clipboard watcher slept while only a KDE device was connected. Fresh 3c review (0 Critical, 3 Important), fixed: Ring sent a type KDE Connect does not know (now `kdeconnect.findmyphone.request`, read from its app; it rang the A71), file intake unbounded and unpaced, and a file outliving its permission; plus a device unpaired while away now told so, `payloadSize: -1`, the part out of Downloads, one switch per request, deadlines on the link's writes. Found on the re-check: the battery sent as a device accepts was dropped during the owner's Confirm (now kept). Owed: the desktop's KDE section on screen, Linux (checklist §87).

  - **How:** computer A pastes B's pairing link (B's Pair tab: "Copy the link for another computer") into Settings → Computers; A's server pins B's certificate from the link for each address, pairs over TLS with `kdeconnect.pair {pin, computer: true}` (B gives A a computer's narrow permissions: Clipboard, Files, Links, ask first; the Devices tab marks it) and names its iroh key with `wan_key`. A keeps one link to B over iroh from its own WAN endpoint (`server/src/peer_computers.rs`, `IrohControlStream::dial_from`), as B's client. 2g: with `computer_clipboard` on on both (off by default), copies cross both ways. 2h: files both ways on the link (A shares as a phone; B's offers A fetches), from the Files tab or `pocketdesk send <computer>`; a folder from B to A; A to B not yet.
  - **Tested:** `server/tests/two_computers.rs` runs two real servers (R6a's seams, plus `POCKETDESK_CLIPBOARD_FILE` and `POCKETDESK_DOWNLOAD_DIR`): a forged certificate and a wrong PIN pair nothing, the code pairs, the link comes up, a copy each way and a pairing link kept back, 3 MB each way and a 5-file folder byte for byte, pairing again, Forget.
  - **Fresh review (0 Critical, 5 Important), fixed one commit each:** a folder from B lost most of its files (two transfers per device; now one at a time per link, busy refusals retried), the packets test failed, pairing again kept a stale link, the copied pairing link (with its PIN) was pushed to every phone's clipboard, and `computer_clipboard` off still let a computer read the clipboard and send images; plus `wan_key` from phones, the link's id and name, the endpoint, link state, the seams, name collisions, links to a computer, and the switch on both sides.
  - **Owed:** two real computers (checklist §86; the VM as B). Known: a computer from before 2f pairs A as a phone; Forget does not unpair on the other side.

A failed send or download now first checks for a network that can reach the computer (Wi-Fi, Ethernet, the phone's own hotspot or tethering; mobile data with the internet only for a computer reachable from away) and waits for one, 3 minutes per outage across all jobs (`TransferRetry`, `OfflineBudget`, JVM-tested), then says "No network for 3 minutes"; a download refused for want of the home network is retried. Fresh review (0 Critical, 3 Important), fixed: the wait was per job (a folder of 20 offline waited an hour), IMS/MMS networks counted, and mobile data counted for a home-only computer.
